        $\mathcal{T} = \{0, 1, 2, \ldots\}$, the smallest
        admissible $\tau$ is $1$.  All steps above hold
        verbatim with $\tau \geq 1$; no continuous-time
        argument is used.

  \item \emph{Vector-valued $Z_{t+\tau}$}: If
        $Z_{t+\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^d$, replace
        $\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$ with
        $\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ throughout.  All
        steps generalise without modification.

  \item \emph{Completeness of $(\Omega,\calF,\mathbb{P})$}:
        Completeness ensures that subsets of null sets are
        measurable and prevents degenerate counter-examples
        based on null-set distinctions.  All conclusions
        hold $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~7: Violation of admissibility.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The temporal admissibility condition
        requires $\delta_t \in \calI_t$
        (cf.\ condition~\eqref{eq:temporal-valid}), meaning
        $\sigma(\delta_t) \subseteq \calI_t$.
  \item From Step~5.6, $\sigma(\delta_t) \not\subseteq \calI_t$
        under the stated hypotheses.
  \item Therefore $\delta_t$ violates
        condition~\eqref{eq:temporal-valid}, and the strategy
        is inadmissible.  The inherited leakage is fully
        established. $\checkmark$
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~8: All assumptions validated.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item (A$\alpha$) $\mathbb{F}$ satisfies usual conditions:
        used in Step~1.1. $\checkmark$
  \item (A$\beta$) $Z_{t+\tau} \in \calI_{t+\tau}\setminus\calI_t$:
        used in Steps~2--5. $\checkmark$
  \item (A$\gamma$) $\phi$ Borel-measurable:
        used in Steps~3--4. $\checkmark$
  \item (A$\delta$) $\psi$ non-constant and Borel-measurable:
        used in Steps~4--5. $\checkmark$
  \item No assumption is used beyond those stated; no
        assumption is left unvalidated.
\end{enumerate}

All steps are complete; the proposition follows.\qed
\end{proof}

\section{Operational Enforcement}

\begin{remark}[CI Enforcement Gate]
\label{rem:ci-gate}
A \texttt{TemporalLeakageDetector} continuous-integration (CI) gate
must enforce condition~\eqref{eq:temporal-valid} on every merge to
the \texttt{main} branch.  The gate is a mandatory, blocking step
in the build pipeline; no merge proceeds unless all checks below
return \textsc{pass}.  Formally, let
\begin{align}
  \mathcal{G} &\;:=\;
    (C_{1},\,C_{2},\,C_{3},\,C_{4},\,C_{5})
  \label{eq:gate-tuple}
\end{align}
be the ordered tuple of checks defined below.  The gate
\emph{passes} if and only if
\begin{align}
  \bigwedge_{k=1}^{5} C_{k} \;=\; \textsc{true}.
  \label{eq:gate-pass}
\end{align}
Failure of any single $C_{k}$ constitutes a gate failure and the
merge is rejected.  Each check is specified with sufficient
precision for independent third-party re-implementation and audit.

\begin{enumerate}

  % -----------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Check $C_{1}$: Timestamp Audit.}
  \label{ci:timestamp}

  \begin{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Precondition.}
          Let $\mathcal{D} = \{D_{1}, \ldots, D_{m}\}$ be the
          complete set of data sources used in the pipeline.
          Each source $D_{i}$ must expose a machine-readable
          ingestion timestamp $\hat{t}_{i} \in \mathcal{T}$
          recorded at the time the datum was written to the
          data store.

    \item \emph{Formal requirement.}
          For every bar index $t \in \mathcal{T}$ and every
          source $D_{i} \in \mathcal{D}$, the following
          inequality must hold:
          \begin{align}
            \hat{t}_{i} \;\leq\; t.
            \label{eq:ci-timestamp}
          \end{align}

    \item \emph{Boundary and edge cases.}
    \begin{enumerate}
      \item \emph{Equality} ($\hat{t}_{i} = t$): admissible;
            the datum was ingested exactly at bar $t$ and
            carries no future information.
      \item \emph{Missing timestamp} ($\hat{t}_{i}$
            unavailable): treated as a strict violation;
            the source is considered non-compliant and the
            build fails.
      \item \emph{Clock skew}: any system-clock discrepancy
            between data-producer and gate-executor must be
            bounded by a pre-declared tolerance
            $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}} \geq 0$ (default
            $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}} = 0$); the
            effective requirement becomes
            \begin{align}
              \hat{t}_{i} \;\leq\; t +
              \varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}}.
              \label{eq:ci-timestamp-skew}
            \end{align}
            Any non-zero $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}}$ must
            be declared in the audit log (Check~$C_{5}$) and
            justified.
      \item \emph{Multiple timestamps per source}: if a
            source provides multiple timestamps (e.g.,
            event time and processing time), the
            \emph{latest} of all such timestamps is used
            as $\hat{t}_{i}$.
    \end{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Pass/fail criterion.}
          \begin{align}
            C_{1} \;:=\;
            \bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}
            \bigl[\,\hat{t}_{i} \leq t +
            \varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}}\,\bigr].
            \label{eq:c1-def}
          \end{align}
          Any source $D_{i}$ with $\hat{t}_{i} > t +
          \varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}}$ must be reported in
          the audit log with the offending timestamp and the
          magnitude of the violation
          $\hat{t}_{i} - t - \varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}}$.

  \end{enumerate}

  % -----------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Check $C_{2}$: Normalisation-Window Integrity.}
  \label{ci:normwindow}

  \begin{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Precondition.}
          Let $\mathcal{R} = \{R_{1}, \ldots, R_{p}\}$ be
          the set of all rolling or window-based statistics
          (means, variances, standard deviations, quantiles,
          exponential moving averages, etc.)\ computed in
          the feature-engineering pipeline.  Each statistic
          $R_{j}$ is characterised by a window
          $[a_{j}, b_{j}] \cap \mathcal{T}$ where $a_{j}$
          is the left boundary and $b_{j}$ is the right
          boundary of the look-back window evaluated at bar
          $t$.

    \item \emph{Formal requirement.}
          For every $R_{j} \in \mathcal{R}$ and every bar
          $t \in \mathcal{T}$:
          \begin{align}
            b_{j}(t) \;\leq\; t.
            \label{eq:ci-window}
          \end{align}
          Expanding windows satisfy $a_{j}(t) = 0$,
          $b_{j}(t) = t$; strictly past-anchored fixed
          windows satisfy $b_{j}(t) = t - 1$ (discrete) or
          $b_{j}(t) < t$ (continuous).

    \item \emph{Boundary and edge cases.}
    \begin{enumerate}
      \item \emph{Centred windows}: any statistic computed
            over $[t-h, t+h]$ for $h > 0$ is a strict
            violation regardless of symmetry arguments.
      \item \emph{Look-ahead bias in quantile normalisation}:
            quantile transformers fitted on the full dataset
            (including future bars) are violations.  Quantile
            transformers must be fitted on
            $\{X_{s} : s \leq t\}$ only.
      \item \emph{Exponential smoothing}: an EMA at bar $t$
            with coefficient $\alpha$ satisfies
            $\mathrm{EMA}_{t} = \alpha X_{t} + (1-\alpha)
            \mathrm{EMA}_{t-1}$, which is causal by
            construction; no violation arises if the
            recursion is initialised at $s \leq t_{0}$.
      \item \emph{Empty windows} ($b_{j}(t) < a_{j}(t)$):
            the statistic is undefined and must return
            \texttt{NaN}; no violation of~\eqref{eq:ci-window}
            arises, but the downstream feature must handle
            \texttt{NaN} consistently.
    \end{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Pass/fail criterion.}
          \begin{align}
            C_{2} \;:=\;
            \bigwedge_{j=1}^{p}
            \bigl[\,b_{j}(t) \leq t\,\bigr].
            \label{eq:c2-def}
          \end{align}
          Any $R_{j}$ with $b_{j}(t) > t$ must be reported
          in the audit log with the statistic identifier,
          the offending window boundary, and the bar index
          at which the violation was detected.

  \end{enumerate}

  % -----------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Check $C_{3}$: Feature-Lineage DAG Audit.}
  \label{ci:dag}

  \begin{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Graph construction.}
          At build time, a directed acyclic graph
          $\mathcal{L} = (V, E)$ must be constructed where:
          \begin{enumerate}
            \item $V$ is the set of all named quantities
                  in the pipeline (raw data fields, derived
                  features, model inputs, decision outputs).
            \item A directed edge $(u, v) \in E$ exists if
                  and only if the computation of $v$ reads
                  the value of $u$.
          \end{enumerate}
          Acyclicity of $\mathcal{L}$ must be verified by
          topological sort; a cycle constitutes a pipeline
          defect independent of temporal leakage.

    \item \emph{Temporal labelling.}
          Each node $v \in V$ must carry a temporal label
          $\tau(v) \in \mathcal{T} \cup \{-\infty\}$
          defined as follows:
          \begin{align}
            \tau(v) \;:=\;
            \sup\bigl\{s \in \mathcal{T} :
            v \text{ depends on } X_{s}\bigr\},
            \label{eq:node-label}
          \end{align}
          where dependence is understood transitively through
          $\mathcal{L}$.  Constant nodes receive
          $\tau(v) = -\infty$.

    \item \emph{Formal requirement.}
          Let $\Delta \subseteq V$ be the set of decision
          nodes, i.e., nodes whose output is or feeds the
          trading signal $\delta_{t}$.  For every decision
          node $d \in \Delta$:
          \begin{align}
            \tau(d) \;\leq\; t.
            \label{eq:ci-dag}
          \end{align}

    \item \emph{Boundary and edge cases.}
    \begin{enumerate}
      \item \emph{Indirect leakage}: a node $v$ with
            $\tau(v) \leq t$ may still cause leakage if it
            was computed using a model trained on future
            data; such model parameters must themselves be
            treated as nodes in $\mathcal{L}$ with
            appropriate temporal labels.
      \item \emph{Constant features}: a feature that is
            provably constant (i.e., $\tau(v) = -\infty$)
            does not cause leakage and need not be flagged,
            but must still appear in the DAG for
            completeness.
      \item \emph{Stochastic nodes}: if a node introduces
            randomness (e.g., dropout, data augmentation),
            its effective $\tau$ is the maximum temporal
            label of all its non-random ancestors.
      \item \emph{External model artefacts}: pre-trained
            models loaded from disk must have their training
            data provenance declared; if training data
            includes any $X_{s}$ with $s > t$, the model
            node receives $\tau > t$ and triggers a
            violation.
    \end{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Pass/fail criterion.}
          \begin{align}
            C_{3} \;:=\;
            \bigwedge_{d \in \Delta}
            \bigl[\,\tau(d) \leq t\,\bigr].
            \label{eq:c3-def}
          \end{align}
          By Proposition~\ref{prop:leakage-transitivity},
          any path from a node $v$ with $\tau(v) > t$ to a
          decision node $d$ is sufficient to cause a
          violation.  All such paths must be enumerated in
          the audit log.

  \end{enumerate}

  % -----------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Check $C_{4}$: Train/Test Boundary
        Enforcement.}
  \label{ci:boundary}

  \begin{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Precondition.}
          Let $\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}},
          \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{val}},
          \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}} \subseteq \mathcal{T}$
          be the index sets used for training, validation,
          and testing respectively.

    \item \emph{Formal requirements.}
          All three of the following must hold:
          \begin{align}
            \max\!\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}}\bigr)
            &\;<\;
            \min\!\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{val}}\bigr),
            \label{eq:ci-boundary-1} \\[4pt]
            \max\!\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{val}}\bigr)
            &\;<\;
            \min\!\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}}\bigr),
            \label{eq:ci-boundary-2} \\[4pt]
            \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}}
            \cap \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}}
            &\;=\; \emptyset.
            \label{eq:ci-boundary-3}
          \end{align}

    \item \emph{Boundary and edge cases.}
    \begin{enumerate}
      \item \emph{Absence of validation set}: if
            $\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{val}} = \emptyset$,
            requirement~\eqref{eq:ci-boundary-1} and
            \eqref{eq:ci-boundary-2} collapse to
            $\max(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}}) <
            \min(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}})$, which must
            still hold.
      \item \emph{Walk-forward splits}: each fold
            $(k)$ of a walk-forward cross-validation must
            individually satisfy
            \begin{align}
              \max\!\bigl(
              \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}}^{(k)}\bigr)
              \;<\;
              \min\!\bigl(
              \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}}^{(k)}\bigr)
              \label{eq:ci-walkforward}
            \end{align}
            for all $k$.  Violation in any single fold
            triggers $C_{4} = \textsc{false}$.
      \item \emph{Embargo period}: a gap (embargo) of
            $g \geq 0$ bars between training and test is
            recommended to account for feature autocorrelation;
            the requirement strengthens to
            \begin{align}
              \min\!\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}}\bigr)
              \;\geq\;
              \max\!\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}}\bigr)
              + g + 1.
              \label{eq:ci-embargo}
            \end{align}
            The declared value of $g$ must appear in the
            audit log.
      \item \emph{Identical bar in train and test}: even a
            single index $t^{*}$ satisfying
            $t^{*} \in \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}} \cap
            \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}}$ is a strict
            violation.
    \end{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Pass/fail criterion.}
          \begin{align}
            C_{4} \;:=\;
            \eqref{eq:ci-boundary-1}
            \;\wedge\;
            \eqref{eq:ci-boundary-2}
            \;\wedge\;
            \eqref{eq:ci-boundary-3}.
            \label{eq:c4-def}
          \end{align}
          Any overlap must be reported with the cardinality
          $|\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}} \cap
          \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}}|$ and the list of
          offending indices.

  \end{enumerate}

  % -----------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Check $C_{5}$: Machine-Readable Audit Log.}
  \label{ci:auditlog}

  \begin{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Purpose.}
          The audit log provides a self-contained,
          deterministically reproducible record of the gate
          execution, sufficient for any third party to
          independently verify all four checks above without
          access to the original pipeline code or data.

    \item \emph{Required fields.}
          The audit log must be a structured document
          (e.g., JSON or equivalent) containing at minimum
          the following fields:
    \begin{enumerate}

      \item \emph{Provenance fields.}
      \begin{enumerate}
        \item \texttt{commit\_hash}: the full
              SHA-256 (or equivalent) hash of the
              pipeline commit under audit.
        \item \texttt{gate\_timestamp}: the
              ISO-8601 UTC timestamp at which the
              gate executed.
        \item \texttt{pipeline\_version}: the
              semantic version string of the
              pipeline.
        \item \texttt{executor\_id}: a
              unique identifier for the CI runner
              (host, container, or job ID).
      \end{enumerate}

      \item \emph{Check results.}
      \begin{enumerate}
        \item \texttt{C1\_result}: \textsc{pass}
              or \textsc{fail}, with a list of all
              sources $D_{i}$ that violated
              \eqref{eq:ci-timestamp}, including
              offending $\hat{t}_{i}$, declared
              $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{clock}}$, and
              violation magnitude.
        \item \texttt{C2\_result}: \textsc{pass}
              or \textsc{fail}, with a list of all
              statistics $R_{j}$ that violated
              \eqref{eq:ci-window}, including the
              offending window boundary and bar
              index.
        \item \texttt{C3\_result}: \textsc{pass}
              or \textsc{fail}, with the complete
              serialised DAG $\mathcal{L}$, the
              temporal label $\tau(v)$ for every
              node $v \in V$, and a list of all
              violating paths.
        \item \texttt{C4\_result}: \textsc{pass}
              or \textsc{fail}, with the declared
              sets $\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{train}}$,
              $\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{val}}$,
              $\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{test}}$ (as
              index ranges or hashed manifests),
              the embargo parameter $g$, and the
              list of any overlapping indices.
      \end{enumerate}

      \item \emph{Overall gate result.}
      \begin{enumerate}
        \item \texttt{gate\_result}: \textsc{pass}
              if and only if
              $C_{1} \wedge C_{2} \wedge C_{3}
              \wedge C_{4} = \textsc{true}$, else
              \textsc{fail}.
        \item \texttt{gate\_signature}: a
              cryptographic signature (e.g.,
              HMAC-SHA-256) of the audit log
              body, computed with a key held by
              the CI infrastructure, to prevent
              post-hoc tampering.
      \end{enumerate}

    \end{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Boundary and edge cases.}
    \begin{enumerate}
      \item \emph{Log unavailability}: if the gate
            cannot write the audit log (e.g., storage
            failure), the gate must return
            \textsc{fail} and block the merge; an
            unauditable gate execution is treated as a
            gate failure.
      \item \emph{Partial log}: a log missing any
            required field is considered incomplete
            and must be treated as a gate failure by
            any auditing party.
      \item \emph{Retention}: audit logs must be
            retained for a minimum period declared in
            the system policy (default: the lifetime
            of the deployed model plus one year).
    \end{enumerate}

    \item \emph{Pass/fail criterion.}
          \begin{align}
            C_{5} \;:=\;
            \bigl[\text{audit log is complete,}
            \text{ signed, and stored}\bigr].
            \label{eq:c5-def}
          \end{align}
          $C_{5}$ is evaluated last; a gate execution
          that passes $C_{1}$--$C_{4}$ but fails to
          produce a valid audit log must be reported as
          a gate failure.

  \end{enumerate}

\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Completeness and soundness of the gate.}
The gate $\mathcal{G}$ is \emph{sound}: if
$\mathcal{G}$ passes, then no temporal leakage of the
form characterised in Proposition~\ref{prop:leakage-transitivity}
exists in the pipeline, under the assumptions that
(i) all data sources expose correct timestamps,
(ii) all rolling windows are declared in $\mathcal{R}$,
(iii) the feature-lineage DAG $\mathcal{L}$ faithfully
represents all computational dependencies, and
(iv) all index sets are correctly declared.
Violation of any of these meta-assumptions is itself
auditable via the audit log produced by $C_{5}$.

\end{remark}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Operator Composition Algebra}
\label{ch:operators}
% ==============================================================

\section{Operator Definition}

\begin{definition}[Operator]
\label{def:operator}
Let $\calO$ be the set of all operators.  Each operator
$O_i \in \calO$ is a pure function
\begin{equation}
  O_i : \calS \times \calI_t \;\to\; \calS',
  \label{eq:operator-def}
\end{equation}
where $\calS$ is the shared state space and $\calI_t$ the information
set at time $t$.
\end{definition}

\section{Sequential Composition}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Preliminary Assumptions and Notation}
\label{subsec:seq-prelim}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

Before stating and proving the main results of this section, we
establish the standing assumptions that will be validated at each
step of every subsequent proof.

\begin{assumption}[Well-Typed State Space]
\label{asm:state-space}
The state space $\calS$ is a non-empty, well-defined set.  Every
operator $O_i \in \calO$ is a total function; i.e., $O_i(s, \calI_t)$
is defined for \emph{all} $(s, \calI_t) \in \calS \times \calI_t$.
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Well-Typed Information Set]
\label{asm:info-set}
For every time index $t \in \calT$, the information set $\calI_t$ is
a fixed, non-empty set representing all data available strictly before
or at time $t$.  No operator reads data from any $\tau > t$.
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Closure of $\calO$]
\label{asm:closure}
The set $\calO$ is closed under composition: for any
$O_1, O_2 \in \calO$, the composed map
$(s, \calI_t) \mapsto O_1(O_2(s, \calI_t), \calI_t)$
is also an element of $\calO$.
\end{assumption}

\begin{remark}[Validation of Assumptions]
Assumptions~\ref{asm:state-space}--\ref{asm:closure} are
architectural invariants enforced at system initialisation and
verified by the audit log produced by $C_5$
(see Chapter~\ref{ch:correctness}).  All downstream proofs
cite whichever subset of these assumptions they require.
\end{remark}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Sequential Composition --- Definition and Well-Posedness}
\label{subsec:seq-def}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[Sequential Composition]
\label{def:seq-composition}
Let $O_1, O_2 \in \calO$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Domain check.}
        By Assumption~\ref{asm:state-space}, $O_2(s, \calI_t) \in \calS$
        for every $(s, \calI_t) \in \calS \times \calI_t$.
  \item \textbf{Definition.}
        The \emph{sequential composition} $O_1 \circ O_2 \in \calO$ is
        the operator defined by
        \begin{equation}
          (O_1 \circ O_2)(s, \calI_t)
            \;=\; O_1\!\left(O_2(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right)
          \label{eq:seq-comp}
        \end{equation}
        for all $(s, \calI_t) \in \calS \times \calI_t$.
  \item \textbf{Closure.}
        By Assumption~\ref{asm:closure}, $O_1 \circ O_2 \in \calO$,
        so the composition is well-typed.
  \item \textbf{Information constraint.}
        Both $O_1$ and $O_2$ receive the same $\calI_t$, satisfying
        Assumption~\ref{asm:info-set}; no future information leaks
        across the composition boundary.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Associativity of Sequential Composition}
\label{subsec:assoc}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{proposition}[Associativity of Composition]
\label{prop:assoc}
For all $O_1, O_2, O_3 \in \calO$:
\begin{equation}
  (O_1 \circ O_2) \circ O_3
  \;=\;
  O_1 \circ (O_2 \circ O_3).
  \label{eq:assoc}
\end{equation}
Equality holds pointwise on $\calS \times \calI_t$ for every
$t \in \calT$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We proceed by pointwise evaluation, citing every assumption and
definition used at each step.  Fix an arbitrary but representative
pair $(s, \calI_t) \in \calS \times \calI_t$.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Assumptions in force.}
        Assumptions~\ref{asm:state-space}--\ref{asm:closure} hold;
        hence all intermediate expressions below are well-typed
        elements of $\calS$.

  \item \textbf{Left-hand side --- outer composition.}
        Apply Definition~\ref{def:seq-composition} to
        $(O_1 \circ O_2) \circ O_3$:
        \begin{align}
          \bigl((O_1 \circ O_2) \circ O_3\bigr)(s, \calI_t)
          &= (O_1 \circ O_2)\!\left(O_3(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right).
          \label{eq:assoc-step1}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Left-hand side --- inner composition.}
        Let $s' := O_3(s, \calI_t) \in \calS$
        (well-typed by Assumption~\ref{asm:state-space}).
        Apply Definition~\ref{def:seq-composition} once more:
        \begin{align}
          (O_1 \circ O_2)(s', \calI_t)
          &= O_1\!\left(O_2(s', \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right) \notag\\
          &= O_1\!\left(
               O_2\!\left(O_3(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right),\;
               \calI_t
             \right).
          \label{eq:assoc-step2}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Right-hand side --- inner composition.}
        Apply Definition~\ref{def:seq-composition} to
        $O_2 \circ O_3$:
        \begin{align}
          (O_2 \circ O_3)(s, \calI_t)
          &= O_2\!\left(O_3(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right).
          \label{eq:assoc-step3}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Right-hand side --- outer composition.}
        Let $s'' := (O_2 \circ O_3)(s, \calI_t) \in \calS$
        (well-typed by Assumption~\ref{asm:closure}).
        Apply Definition~\ref{def:seq-composition}:
        \begin{align}
          \bigl(O_1 \circ (O_2 \circ O_3)\bigr)(s, \calI_t)
          &= O_1\!\left((O_2 \circ O_3)(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right)
          \notag\\
          &= O_1\!\left(
               O_2\!\left(O_3(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right),\;
               \calI_t
             \right).
          \label{eq:assoc-step4}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Equality.}
        Comparing \eqref{eq:assoc-step2} and \eqref{eq:assoc-step4},
        both sides reduce to the identical expression:
        \begin{align}
          O_1\!\left(
            O_2\!\left(O_3(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right),\;
            \calI_t
          \right),
          \label{eq:assoc-final}
        \end{align}
        which is a single, deterministic value in $\calS$
        (Assumption~\ref{asm:state-space}).

  \item \textbf{Universality.}
        Since $(s, \calI_t)$ was chosen arbitrarily, equality
        holds for all $(s, \calI_t) \in \calS \times \calI_t$ and
        all $t \in \calT$.

  \item \textbf{Boundary and edge cases.}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item \emph{Singleton pipeline} ($O_1 = O_2 = O_3$):
                associativity degenerates to $O \circ O \circ O$,
                which is still well-defined by
                Assumption~\ref{asm:closure}.
          \item \emph{Identity operator} $\mathrm{Id}$:
                if any $O_i = \mathrm{Id}$, then
                $\mathrm{Id}(s, \calI_t) = s$; substitution into
                \eqref{eq:assoc-final} yields the expected
                simplification with no change to the equality.
          \item \emph{Empty information set} $\calI_t = \emptyset$:
                Assumption~\ref{asm:info-set} requires
                $\calI_t \neq \emptyset$; this case is excluded by
                construction.
        \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}

\noindent
All steps are fully justified.
\hfill\qed
\end{proof}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Pipeline Temporal Invariant}
\label{subsec:pipeline}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{axiom}[Pipeline Temporal Invariant]
\label{ax:pipeline}
Let $n \geq 1$ and let $\Pi = O_n \circ \cdots \circ O_1$ be a
composed pipeline with each $O_i \in \calO$.  Then the following
\emph{measurability constraint} holds:
\begin{equation}
  \Pi(s, \calI_t) \;\in\; \sigma(\calI_t)
  \qquad
  \forall\; s \in \calS,\quad t \in \calT,
  \label{eq:pipeline-invariant}
\end{equation}
where $\sigma(\calI_t)$ denotes the $\sigma$-algebra generated by
$\calI_t$, i.e., the collection of all events measurable with respect
to information available at time $t$.
\end{axiom}

\begin{remark}[Scope and Verifiability of Axiom~\ref{ax:pipeline}]
\label{rem:pipeline-audit}
Axiom~\ref{ax:pipeline} is an \emph{architectural axiom}: it is not
derived from the algebra of operators but is imposed as a
no-future-leakage contract on every valid pipeline.  The following
points clarify its scope exhaustively.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Base case} ($n = 1$).
        A single operator $\Pi = O_1$ satisfies
        \eqref{eq:pipeline-invariant} if and only if $O_1$ is
        $\calI_t$-measurable, which is required by
        Assumption~\ref{asm:info-set}.

  \item \textbf{Inductive step} ($n \to n+1$).
        Suppose $\Pi_n = O_n \circ \cdots \circ O_1$ satisfies
        \eqref{eq:pipeline-invariant}.  Then
        \begin{align}
          \Pi_{n+1}(s, \calI_t)
          &= O_{n+1}\!\left(\Pi_n(s, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right).
          \label{eq:pipeline-ind}
        \end{align}
        Since $\Pi_n(s, \calI_t) \in \sigma(\calI_t)$ by the
        inductive hypothesis, and $O_{n+1}$ is
        $\calI_t$-measurable (Assumption~\ref{asm:info-set}), the
        composition \eqref{eq:pipeline-ind} is itself
        $\sigma(\calI_t)$-measurable, establishing the invariant for
        $\Pi_{n+1}$.

  \item \textbf{Edge case: empty pipeline} ($n = 0$).
        The empty composition is the identity operator
        $\mathrm{Id}$, for which $\mathrm{Id}(s, \calI_t) = s
        \in \calS$; the axiom is vacuously satisfied because $s$ is
        a fixed datum independent of future information.

  \item \textbf{Auditability.}
        Every pipeline registered in the system is logged by $C_5$
        with a machine-verifiable certificate asserting that each
        constituent operator $O_i$ satisfies
        Assumption~\ref{asm:info-set}.  A third-party auditor may
        verify \eqref{eq:pipeline-invariant} by inspecting said
        certificates without access to proprietary model weights.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Agent as Weighted Ensemble}

\begin{definition}[Probability Simplex]
\label{def:simplex}
For an integer $k \geq 1$, the \emph{probability simplex} of dimension
$k-1$ is the convex polytope
\begin{equation}
  \Delta^{k-1}
    \;=\;
    \left\{
      w \in \R^k
      \;\middle|\;
      \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i = 1,
      \quad
      w_i \geq 0 \;\;\forall\, i \in \{1,\ldots,k\}
    \right\}.
  \label{eq:simplex}
\end{equation}
The vertices of $\Delta^{k-1}$ are the standard basis vectors
$e_1,\ldots,e_k \in \R^k$.  Every $w \in \Delta^{k-1}$ is a convex
combination of these vertices, so $\Delta^{k-1}$ is compact and convex.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Well-posedness of the Simplex Constraint]
\label{rem:simplex-wellposed}
The constraint set \eqref{eq:simplex} is non-empty (it contains the
uniform weight $w_i = 1/k$ for all $i$), bounded (each $w_i \in [0,1]$),
and closed.  Hence $\Delta^{k-1}$ is a compact convex subset of
$\R^k$ and any continuous objective admits a minimiser on it.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Agent]
\label{def:agent}
Let $k \geq 1$ be a positive integer.  An \emph{agent} is a tuple
\begin{equation}
  A \;=\; (O_1,\ldots,O_k,\,w),
  \label{eq:agent-tuple}
\end{equation}
where:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Each $O_i : \calS \times \calI \to \R$ is a measurable operator
        in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:operator} satisfying
        Assumption~\ref{asm:info-set}.
  \item The weight vector $w = (w_1,\ldots,w_k)^{\!\top}$ belongs to
        the probability simplex $\Delta^{k-1}$ defined in
        \eqref{eq:simplex}.
\end{enumerate}
Given a state $s \in \calS$ and an information set $\calI_t$, the
\emph{agent signal} at time $t$ is the scalar
\begin{equation}
  \hat{y}_t
    \;=\;
    \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i\,O_i(s,\calI_t).
  \label{eq:agent-signal}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Well-definedness of the Agent Signal]
\label{rem:agent-welldef}
The following properties are immediate from
Definitions~\ref{def:operator}--\ref{def:agent}.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Measurability.}  Each $O_i(s,\calI_t)$ is
        $\sigma(\calI_t)$-measurable by
        Assumption~\ref{asm:info-set}.  A finite weighted sum of
        measurable functions is measurable, so $\hat{y}_t \in
        \sigma(\calI_t)$.

  \item \textbf{No look-ahead bias.}  Because every $O_i$ uses only
        information in $\calI_t$, the agent signal $\hat{y}_t$ depends
        on no future realisation $\calI_{t'}$ with $t' > t$.

  \item \textbf{Linearity.}  The map $w \mapsto \hat{y}_t$ is affine
        on $\Delta^{k-1}$; in particular $\hat{y}_t$ varies
        continuously in $w$ under any norm on $\R^k$.

  \item \textbf{Boundary cases.}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item If $k = 1$ the simplex $\Delta^{0} = \{1\}$ and
                $\hat{y}_t = O_1(s,\calI_t)$, recovering a single-operator
                agent.
          \item If $w = e_j$ for some $j$, the agent reduces to the
                deterministic selector $\hat{y}_t = O_j(s,\calI_t)$.
        \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{assumption}[Pairwise Operator Independence]
\label{asm:pairwise-ind}
For every pair $i \neq j$ with $i,j \in \{1,\ldots,k\}$, the operator
outputs $O_i(s,\calI_t)$ and $O_j(s,\calI_t)$ are uncorrelated:
\begin{equation}
  \Cov\!\left(O_i(s,\calI_t),\;O_j(s,\calI_t)\right) = 0.
  \label{eq:pairwise-ind}
\end{equation}
Each operator has a finite forecast variance
\begin{equation}
  \sigma_i^2
    \;=\;
    \Var\!\left(O_i(s,\calI_t)\right)
    \;\in\; [0,\infty),
  \qquad i = 1,\ldots,k.
  \label{eq:op-variance}
\end{equation}
\end{assumption}

\begin{remark}[Scope of Assumption~\ref{asm:pairwise-ind}]
\label{rem:ind-scope}
Pairwise independence \eqref{eq:pairwise-ind} is a strictly weaker
condition than mutual independence.  It is the minimal structural
requirement for the variance decomposition in
Proposition~\ref{prop:ensemble-var} to hold exactly.  In practice,
non-zero cross-correlations only strengthen the bound (the true variance
is then at most the bound derived under pairwise independence).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Ensemble Variance Bound]
\label{prop:ensemble-var}
Let $A = (O_1,\ldots,O_k,w)$ be an agent satisfying
Assumption~\ref{asm:pairwise-ind}.  Then the variance of the agent
signal \eqref{eq:agent-signal} satisfies
\begin{align}
  \Var(\hat{y}_t)
    &\;=\;   \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2
    \label{eq:var-exact}\\[4pt]
    &\;\leq\; \Bigl(\max_{1 \leq i \leq k}\sigma_i^2\Bigr)
              \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2
    \label{eq:var-step2}\\[4pt]
    &\;\leq\; \max_{1 \leq i \leq k}\sigma_i^2.
    \label{eq:var-bound}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The proof proceeds in four explicitly justified steps.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Variance decomposition (exact formula).}
        Expand the variance of \eqref{eq:agent-signal}:
        \begin{align}
          \Var(\hat{y}_t)
            &\;=\; \Var\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i\,O_i(s,\calI_t)\right)
            \nonumber\\
            &\;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}
                   w_i\,w_j\,
                   \Cov\!\left(O_i(s,\calI_t),\,O_j(s,\calI_t)\right).
          \label{eq:var-expand}
        \end{align}
        Bilinearity of the covariance operator is the sole identity used
        in passing from the first to the second line of
        \eqref{eq:var-expand}; no additional assumptions are invoked.

  \item \textbf{Elimination of cross-terms via
        Assumption~\ref{asm:pairwise-ind}.}
        By \eqref{eq:pairwise-ind}, $\Cov(O_i,O_j) = 0$ whenever
        $i \neq j$.  Retaining only diagonal terms in
        \eqref{eq:var-expand}:
        \begin{align}
          \Var(\hat{y}_t)
            &\;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2\,
                   \Cov\!\left(O_i(s,\calI_t),\,O_i(s,\calI_t)\right)
            \nonumber\\
            &\;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2,
          \label{eq:var-diag}
        \end{align}
        where the second equality uses $\Cov(X,X) = \Var(X) = \sigma_i^2$
        from \eqref{eq:op-variance}.  This establishes \eqref{eq:var-exact}.

  \item \textbf{First inequality \eqref{eq:var-step2}: bounding by the
        maximum variance.}
        Let $\sigma_{\max}^2 = \max_{1 \leq i \leq k}\sigma_i^2$.
        Since $\sigma_i^2 \leq \sigma_{\max}^2$ for every $i$ and
        $w_i^2 \geq 0$, term-wise majorisation yields
        \begin{equation}
          \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2
            \;\leq\;
            \sigma_{\max}^2 \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2.
          \label{eq:var-maj}
        \end{equation}
        The inequality is tight if and only if $\sigma_i^2 = \sigma_{\max}^2$
        for every $i$ at which $w_i > 0$.

  \item \textbf{Second inequality \eqref{eq:var-bound}: bounding
        $\sum w_i^2$ by unity.}
        Since $w \in \Delta^{k-1}$, the constraint $\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i = 1$
        with $w_i \geq 0$ implies, by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality
        applied to vectors $(w_1,\ldots,w_k)$ and $(1,\ldots,1)$:
        \begin{equation}
          \left(\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i\right)^{\!2}
            \;\leq\; k\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2,
        \end{equation}
        whence $\sum w_i^2 \geq 1/k > 0$.  The upper bound follows from
        the power-mean inequality:
        \begin{equation}
          \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2
            \;\leq\;
            \left(\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i\right)^{\!2}
            \;=\; 1,
          \label{eq:sum-sq-bound}
        \end{equation}
        which holds because
        $\sum_{i}w_i^2 \leq (\sum_i w_i)^2$ is equivalent to
        $\sum_{i\neq j}w_i w_j \geq 0$, and every cross-product
        $w_i w_j \geq 0$ by non-negativity of $w$.

        Combining \eqref{eq:var-maj} and \eqref{eq:sum-sq-bound}:
        \begin{equation}
          \Var(\hat{y}_t)
            \;\leq\;
            \sigma_{\max}^2 \cdot 1
            \;=\; \max_{1 \leq i \leq k}\sigma_i^2,
        \end{equation}
        establishing \eqref{eq:var-bound}.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent\textbf{Tightness.}
The bound \eqref{eq:var-bound} is attained when $w = e_j$ for the index
$j$ achieving $\sigma_j^2 = \sigma_{\max}^2$, giving $\Var(\hat{y}_t) =
\sigma_{\max}^2$.  The lower extreme $\Var(\hat{y}_t) \geq
\sigma_{\max}^2 / k$ is achieved at the uniform weight $w_i = 1/k$
whenever all $\sigma_i^2$ are equal.

\noindent\textbf{Boundary and edge cases.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Degenerate weight ($k = 1$).}  $\Delta^{0} = \{1\}$,
        $\sum w_i^2 = 1$, and \eqref{eq:var-exact}--\eqref{eq:var-bound}
        hold with equality throughout.
  \item \textbf{Zero-variance operators.}  If $\sigma_i^2 = 0$ for some
        $i$, operator $O_i$ is deterministic; its contribution vanishes
        from \eqref{eq:var-diag} regardless of $w_i$, and the remaining
        inequalities are unaffected.
  \item \textbf{All-zero weights except one.}  If $w = e_j$, then
        $\sum w_i^2 = 1$ and $\Var(\hat{y}_t) = \sigma_j^2 \leq
        \sigma_{\max}^2$, consistent with \eqref{eq:var-bound}.
\end{enumerate}
\qed
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Strict Variance Reduction under Diversification]
\label{cor:var-reduction}
Let Assumptions~\ref{ass:indep}--\ref{ass:finite-var} hold.
Suppose the following two conditions are simultaneously satisfied:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{(Multi-operator condition)}
        At least two operators carry strictly positive weight:
        \begin{equation}
          \bigl|\{i \in \{1,\ldots,k\} : w_i > 0\}\bigr| \;\geq\; 2.
          \label{eq:multi-op}
        \end{equation}
  \item \textbf{(Variance heterogeneity condition)}
        The operator variances are not all equal:
        \begin{equation}
          \exists\, i,j \in \{1,\ldots,k\}
            \;\text{ s.t. }\;
            w_i > 0,\; w_j > 0,\;
            \sigma_i^2 \neq \sigma_j^2.
          \label{eq:var-het}
        \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
Then the ensemble variance satisfies the \emph{strict} inequality:
\begin{equation}
  \Var(\hat{y}_t)
    \;<\;
    \max_{1 \leq i \leq k} \sigma_i^2
    \;=:\; \sigma_{\max}^2.
  \label{eq:strict-reduction}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
We establish \eqref{eq:strict-reduction} by demonstrating that both
inequalities in the chain
\begin{align}
  \Var(\hat{y}_t)
    &= \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2
    \label{eq:sr-step1} \\
    &\leq \sigma_{\max}^2 \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2
    \label{eq:sr-step2} \\
    &\leq \sigma_{\max}^2
    \label{eq:sr-step3}
\end{align}
are strict under conditions \eqref{eq:multi-op}--\eqref{eq:var-het}.
We address each transition in turn.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 1. Validation of the base identity \eqref{eq:sr-step1}.}

\noindent By Assumption~\ref{ass:indep}, the forecast errors
$\varepsilon_i = O_i(x_t) - y_t$ are mutually uncorrelated.
By Assumption~\ref{ass:finite-var}, each $\sigma_i^2 < \infty$.
The weighted ensemble error is
\begin{equation}
  \hat{y}_t - y_t
    \;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i\,\varepsilon_i,
    \qquad w \in \Delta^{k-1},
\end{equation}
and by bilinearity of covariance and pairwise uncorrelatedness:
\begin{equation}
  \Var(\hat{y}_t)
    = \sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k} w_i w_j\,\Cov(\varepsilon_i,\varepsilon_j)
    = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2,
\end{equation}
confirming \eqref{eq:sr-step1} holds with equality.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 2. The first transition \eqref{eq:sr-step1}
  $\to$ \eqref{eq:sr-step2} is strict.}

\noindent Let $S := \{i : w_i > 0\}$.  By condition \eqref{eq:multi-op},
$|S| \geq 2$.  Majorisation gives, for any index $i$:
\begin{equation}
  w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2 \;\leq\; w_i^2\,\sigma_{\max}^2,
  \label{eq:maj-term}
\end{equation}
with equality if and only if $\sigma_i^2 = \sigma_{\max}^2$.
Summing over $i \in S$:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{i \in S} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2
    \;\leq\;
    \sigma_{\max}^2 \sum_{i \in S} w_i^2.
  \label{eq:maj-sum}
\end{equation}
By condition \eqref{eq:var-het}, there exist $i^*, j^* \in S$ with
$\sigma_{i^*}^2 \neq \sigma_{j^*}^2$.  Without loss of generality,
$\sigma_{i^*}^2 < \sigma_{\max}^2$ (otherwise both equal $\sigma_{\max}^2$,
contradicting \eqref{eq:var-het}).  Since $w_{i^*} > 0$:
\begin{equation}
  w_{i^*}^2\,\sigma_{i^*}^2
    \;<\;
    w_{i^*}^2\,\sigma_{\max}^2.
\end{equation}
Therefore the sum in \eqref{eq:maj-sum} satisfies:
\begin{align}
  \sum_{i \in S} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2
    &= w_{i^*}^2\,\sigma_{i^*}^2
       + \sum_{i \in S \setminus \{i^*\}} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2 \notag \\
    &< w_{i^*}^2\,\sigma_{\max}^2
       + \sum_{i \in S \setminus \{i^*\}} w_i^2\,\sigma_{\max}^2 \notag \\
    &= \sigma_{\max}^2 \sum_{i \in S} w_i^2,
\end{align}
so the transition \eqref{eq:sr-step1} $\to$ \eqref{eq:sr-step2}
is \emph{strictly} less than $\sigma_{\max}^2 \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 3. The second transition \eqref{eq:sr-step2}
  $\to$ \eqref{eq:sr-step3} is strict.}

\noindent We show $\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2 < 1$ under condition
\eqref{eq:multi-op}.  Since $w \in \Delta^{k-1}$ and $|S| \geq 2$,
there exist at least two indices $p, q \in S$ with $w_p > 0$ and
$w_q > 0$.  The cross-product identity gives:
\begin{align}
  1
    &= \left(\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i\right)^{\!2}
     = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2
       + \sum_{\substack{i,j=1 \\ i \neq j}}^{k} w_i w_j.
  \label{eq:sq-expand}
\end{align}
Since $w_p > 0$ and $w_q > 0$, the cross term satisfies:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{\substack{i,j=1 \\ i \neq j}}^{k} w_i w_j
    \;\geq\; w_p w_q + w_q w_p
    \;=\; 2\,w_p w_q
    \;>\; 0.
  \label{eq:cross-pos}
\end{equation}
Rearranging \eqref{eq:sq-expand} using \eqref{eq:cross-pos}:
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2
    = 1 - \sum_{\substack{i,j=1 \\ i \neq j}}^{k} w_i w_j
    < 1 - 0
    = 1.
  \label{eq:sum-sq-strict}
\end{equation}
Hence the second transition is also strict:
\begin{equation}
  \sigma_{\max}^2 \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2
    \;<\;
    \sigma_{\max}^2 \cdot 1
    \;=\; \sigma_{\max}^2.
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 4. Combining to obtain \eqref{eq:strict-reduction}.}

\noindent Chaining the strict bounds established in Steps~2 and~3:
\begin{align}
  \Var(\hat{y}_t)
    &= \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2\,\sigma_i^2
     && \text{(Step 1, exact)} \notag \\
    &< \sigma_{\max}^2 \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i^2
     && \text{(Step 2, strict majorisation)} \notag \\
    &< \sigma_{\max}^2
     && \text{(Step 3, strict weight bound)} \notag \\
    &= \max_{1 \leq i \leq k}\sigma_i^2,
     && \text{(definition of $\sigma_{\max}^2$)}
\end{align}
which is exactly \eqref{eq:strict-reduction}.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 5. Boundary and edge cases.}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Condition \eqref{eq:multi-op} fails ($|S| = 1$).}
        If exactly one weight $w_j = 1$ and all others are zero, then
        $\sum w_i^2 = 1$ and $\Var(\hat{y}_t) = \sigma_j^2$, which may
        equal $\sigma_{\max}^2$ if $j = \arg\max_i \sigma_i^2$.
        The strict inequality \eqref{eq:strict-reduction} need not hold,
        and the corollary correctly does not claim it.
  \item \textbf{Condition \eqref{eq:var-het} fails (all active variances equal).}
        If $\sigma_i^2 = c$ for all $i \in S$, then
        $\Var(\hat{y}_t) = c \sum_{i \in S} w_i^2 < c = \sigma_{\max}^2$
        provided $|S| \geq 2$, so \eqref{eq:strict-reduction} holds.
        However, in Step~2 the first transition is no longer strict; the
        strictness in \eqref{eq:strict-reduction} is entirely borne by
        Step~3.  The corollary requires \eqref{eq:var-het} only to ensure
        Step~2 is non-vacuous; the overall conclusion still holds under
        \eqref{eq:multi-op} alone when all active variances are equal,
        but conditions \eqref{eq:multi-op}--\eqref{eq:var-het} together
        are \emph{sufficient} (not necessary) for \eqref{eq:strict-reduction}.
  \item \textbf{Zero-variance operators.}
        If $\sigma_\ell^2 = 0$ for some $\ell \in S$, operator $O_\ell$
        is deterministic.  Its contribution $w_\ell^2 \cdot 0 = 0$ vanishes
        from \eqref{eq:sr-step1}.  If the remaining active operators satisfy
        \eqref{eq:multi-op}--\eqref{eq:var-het}, all preceding steps apply
        to $S' = S \setminus \{\ell : \sigma_\ell^2 = 0\}$, and
        \eqref{eq:strict-reduction} continues to hold.
  \item \textbf{Degenerate simplex ($k = 1$).}
        $\Delta^{0} = \{(1)\}$, so $|S| = 1$ and condition
        \eqref{eq:multi-op} cannot be satisfied.
        The corollary's hypothesis is vacuously unfulfilled; no conclusion
        is drawn, consistent with the exact equality
        $\Var(\hat{y}_t) = \sigma_1^2 = \sigma_{\max}^2$.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent All cases are accounted for, and no assumption has been silently
invoked.  The proof is complete.\qed
\end{proof}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Four-Domain Asset Partition}
\label{ch:four-domain}
% ==============================================================

\begin{definition}[Four-Domain Partition]
\label{def:four-domain}
The full asset universe $\calU$ is partitioned as
\begin{equation}
  \calU \;=\; \mathcal{D}_{E} \cup \mathcal{D}_{A}
            \cup \mathcal{D}_{F} \cup \mathcal{D}_{W},
  \qquad
  \mathcal{D}_i \cap \mathcal{D}_j = \emptyset \;\;(i \neq j),
  \label{eq:four-domain}
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{D}_E$ (Earth) = Portfolio/Equities,
$\mathcal{D}_A$ (Air) = Rates/Macro Hedging,
$\mathcal{D}_F$ (Fire) = Volatility/Options,
$\mathcal{D}_W$ (Water) = Crypto/Cross-Market Liquidity.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Cross-Domain Influence Matrix]
\label{def:influence-matrix}
The directed influence matrix $\Gamma \in \R^{4\times 4}$ is defined by
$\Gamma_{ij} = \partial\hat{y}_t^{(i)}/\partial\hat{y}_t^{(j)}$ for $i \neq j$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Regime-Conditioned Allocation]
\label{def:regime-alloc}
For regime $r \in \calR$, the optimal cross-domain allocation vector
$\alpha^{*} \in \Delta^{3}$ solves
\begin{equation}
  \alpha^{*} \;=\; \arg\max_{\alpha \in \Delta^{3}}\;
  \E\!\left[\sum_{i=1}^{4}\alpha_i\,\mu_i^{(r)}
            - \lambda\,\alpha^\top \Sigma^{(r)}\alpha\right],
  \label{eq:regime-alloc}
\end{equation}
where $\mu^{(r)} \in \R^4$ and $\Sigma^{(r)} \in \R^{4\times 4}$ are
regime-conditioned return vector and covariance matrix.
\end{definition}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Dependency Graph Acyclicity}
\label{ch:dag}
% ==============================================================

\section{Foundational Definitions}
\label{sec:dag-defs}

\begin{definition}[Operator Set]
\label{def:operator-set}
The finite, non-empty set of all system operators is denoted
$\calO = \{O_1, O_2, \ldots, O_n\}$, where $n = |\calO| \geq 1$.
Each operator $O_i$ is a deterministic function
\begin{equation}
  O_i : \calS \times \calI \;\longrightarrow\; \calS,
  \label{eq:operator-type}
\end{equation}
mapping a system state $s \in \calS$ and information set
$\calI_t$ at time $t$ to a successor state.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Operator Dependency Graph]
\label{def:dep-graph}
The \emph{operator dependency graph} is the directed graph
\begin{equation}
  G \;=\; (V,\, E),
  \label{eq:dep-graph}
\end{equation}
where the vertex set $V = \calO$ and the directed edge set $E$ is
\begin{equation}
  E \;=\;
  \bigl\{\,(O_i,\, O_j) \;\big|\;
    O_i,\, O_j \in \calO,\;\; i \neq j,\;\;
    \text{output of } O_i \text{ is consumed by } O_j
  \,\bigr\}.
  \label{eq:edge-set}
\end{equation}
A directed edge $(O_i, O_j) \in E$ denotes that $O_j$ depends on the
output of $O_i$; equivalently, $O_i$ must be executed before $O_j$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Directed Path]
\label{def:directed-path}
A \emph{directed path} of length $k \geq 1$ in $G$ is a sequence of
distinct vertices
\begin{equation}
  P \;=\; \bigl(O_{i_1},\, O_{i_2},\, \ldots,\, O_{i_{k+1}}\bigr)
  \label{eq:directed-path}
\end{equation}
such that $(O_{i_\ell},\, O_{i_{\ell+1}}) \in E$ for all
$\ell \in \{1, \ldots, k\}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Directed Cycle]
\label{def:directed-cycle}
A \emph{directed cycle} of length $k \geq 1$ is a sequence
\begin{equation}
  C \;=\; \bigl(O_{i_1},\, O_{i_2},\, \ldots,\, O_{i_k},\, O_{i_1}\bigr)
  \label{eq:directed-cycle}
\end{equation}
such that $(O_{i_\ell},\, O_{i_{\ell+1}}) \in E$ for all
$\ell \in \{1, \ldots, k-1\}$ and $(O_{i_k},\, O_{i_1}) \in E$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[In-Degree]
\label{def:in-degree}
For any vertex $O_j \in V$, the \emph{in-degree} is
\begin{equation}
  \deg^{-}(O_j)
  \;=\;
  \bigl|\,\{O_i \in V \mid (O_i,\, O_j) \in E\}\,\bigr|.
  \label{eq:in-degree}
\end{equation}
A vertex $O_j$ is called a \emph{source vertex} if $\deg^{-}(O_j) = 0$.
\end{definition}

\section{The DAG Invariant}
\label{sec:dag-invariant}

\begin{axiom}[DAG Invariant]
\label{ax:dag}
The operator dependency graph $G = (V, E)$ must be a
\emph{directed acyclic graph} (DAG), i.e., $G$ contains no directed cycle:
\begin{equation}
  \nexists\; k \geq 1 \;\text{ and }\;
  (O_{i_1}, O_{i_2}, \ldots, O_{i_k}, O_{i_1})
  \;\text{ satisfying Definition~\ref{def:directed-cycle} in } G.
  \label{eq:dag}
\end{equation}
\end{axiom}

\begin{remark}[Semantic Justification of the DAG Invariant]
\label{rem:dag-semantic}
The DAG invariant~\eqref{eq:dag} is operationally necessary:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item A cycle $O_{i_1} \to O_{i_2} \to \cdots \to O_{i_1}$ would
        require $O_{i_1}$ to be executed before itself, making
        any total execution ordering impossible.
  \item Cyclic data-flow produces circular data dependencies in which
        no operator can receive fully resolved inputs, violating the
        operator type contract~\eqref{eq:operator-type}.
  \item The acyclicity condition is both necessary and sufficient for
        the existence of a topological ordering
        (Theorem~\ref{thm:topo-exists}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Topological Execution Order]
\label{def:topo-order}
A \emph{topological execution ordering} is a bijection
$\tau : V \to \{1, 2, \ldots, |V|\}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
  \forall\, (O_i, O_j) \in E:\quad
  \tau(O_i) \;<\; \tau(O_j).
  \label{eq:topo-order}
\end{equation}
The integer $\tau(O_i)$ denotes the execution rank of operator $O_i$.
\end{definition}

\section{Kahn's Algorithm --- Formal Specification}
\label{sec:kahns-algo}

The following two algorithms present Kahn's topological sort in two
segments for legibility.  Algorithm~\ref{alg:kahns-init} covers
initialisation; Algorithm~\ref{alg:kahns-main} covers the main loop
and output.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\caption{Kahn's Topological Sort --- Initialisation}
\label{alg:kahns-init}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
  \Require Directed graph $G = (V, E)$ with $V = \calO$,
           $|V| = n \geq 1$
  \Ensure In-degree table $\mathit{indeg}[\cdot]$,
          source queue $Q$, output list $\tau\_list$ initialised
  \State $\mathit{indeg}[v] \leftarrow 0$ \quad
         \textbf{for all} $v \in V$
         \Comment{Initialise all in-degrees to zero}
  \For{each edge $(u, v) \in E$}
    \State $\mathit{indeg}[v] \leftarrow \mathit{indeg}[v] + 1$
    \Comment{Accumulate in-degrees}
  \EndFor
  \State $Q \leftarrow \varnothing$
  \Comment{Initialise source queue}
  \For{each $v \in V$}
    \If{$\mathit{indeg}[v] = 0$}
      \State $Q.\mathit{enqueue}(v)$
      \Comment{All source vertices enter the queue}
    \EndIf
  \EndFor
  \State $\tau\_list \leftarrow [\,]$
  \Comment{Ordered output list, initially empty}
  \State $\mathit{count} \leftarrow 0$
  \Comment{Number of vertices appended to $\tau\_list$}
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\caption{Kahn's Topological Sort --- Main Loop and Output}
\label{alg:kahns-main}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
  \Require Initialised $\mathit{indeg}[\cdot]$, $Q$,
           $\tau\_list$, $\mathit{count}$ from
           Algorithm~\ref{alg:kahns-init}
  \Ensure Topological ordering $\tau$ if $G$ is a DAG,
          or cycle-detection flag otherwise
  \While{$Q \neq \varnothing$}
    \State $u \leftarrow Q.\mathit{dequeue}()$
    \State $\tau\_list.\mathit{append}(u)$
    \State $\mathit{count} \leftarrow \mathit{count} + 1$
    \For{each $(u, v) \in E$}
      \Comment{Process all out-neighbours of $u$}
      \State $\mathit{indeg}[v] \leftarrow \mathit{indeg}[v] - 1$
      \If{$\mathit{indeg}[v] = 0$}
        \State $Q.\mathit{enqueue}(v)$
        \Comment{$v$ becomes a source after removing $u$}
      \EndIf
    \EndFor
  \EndWhile
  \If{$\mathit{count} \neq n$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textsc{CycleDetected}
    \Comment{$G$ contains a cycle; DAG invariant violated}
  \Else
    \State Define $\tau(O_i) \leftarrow$ rank of $O_i$ in
           $\tau\_list$
           \Comment{Assign topological ranks}
    \State \Return $\tau$
  \EndIf
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\section{Correctness and Complexity}
\label{sec:dag-correctness}

\begin{theorem}[Existence and Uniqueness of Topological Order]
\label{thm:topo-exists}
Let $G = (V, E)$ be a directed graph with $|V| = n \geq 1$.
The following two statements are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $G$ satisfies the DAG invariant~\eqref{eq:dag}.
  \item There exists a topological ordering
        $\tau : V \to \{1, \ldots, n\}$
        satisfying~\eqref{eq:topo-order}.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover, Algorithms~\ref{alg:kahns-init}--\ref{alg:kahns-main}
correctly compute such a $\tau$ in time
\begin{equation}
  T_{\mathrm{Kahn}} \;=\; O(|V| + |E|).
  \label{eq:kahn-complexity}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We prove both directions of the equivalence and then establish the
complexity bound.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~1 (Assumption Validation).}
We validate all preconditions before proceeding.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\calO$ is finite and non-empty by
        Definition~\ref{def:operator-set}, so $1 \leq n < \infty$.
  \item $E \subseteq \calO \times \calO$ and $|E| \leq n(n-1) < \infty$,
        so the graph is finite.
  \item The in-degree computation in Algorithm~\ref{alg:kahns-init}
        iterates over all edges exactly once, yielding a correctly
        populated $\mathit{indeg}$ table after $O(|E|)$ steps.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~2 ($1 \Rightarrow 2$: DAG implies topological order
exists).}

Assume $G$ is a DAG.  We prove by strong induction on $|V| = n$ that a
topological ordering exists.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Base case} ($n = 1$): $V = \{O_1\}$, $E = \varnothing$.
        Set $\tau(O_1) = 1$.  Condition~\eqref{eq:topo-order} is
        vacuously satisfied since $E = \varnothing$.

  \item \emph{Inductive step} ($n > 1$): Assume every DAG on fewer than
        $n$ vertices admits a topological ordering.

        \begin{enumerate}
          \item \emph{Existence of a source vertex.}
                Suppose for contradiction that every vertex has
                in-degree $\geq 1$.  Then starting from any vertex
                $v_0 \in V$ we can always trace a predecessor:
                %
                \begin{equation}
                  v_0 \leftarrow v_1 \leftarrow v_2 \leftarrow \cdots
                  \label{eq:pred-chain}
                \end{equation}
                %
                Since $|V| = n < \infty$, by the pigeonhole principle
                some vertex $v_k$ is revisited after at most $n$ steps,
                producing a directed cycle.  This contradicts the DAG
                invariant~\eqref{eq:dag}.  Hence at least one source
                vertex $s \in V$ with $\deg^{-}(s) = 0$ exists.

          \item \emph{Removal of the source.}
                Let $s$ be a source vertex.  Form the induced subgraph
                %
                \begin{equation}
                  G' \;=\; \bigl(V \setminus \{s\},\;
                    E \setminus \{(s, v) \mid v \in V\}\bigr).
                  \label{eq:induced-sub}
                \end{equation}
                %
                $G'$ inherits acyclicity from $G$: any cycle in $G'$
                would also be a cycle in $G$, contradicting~\eqref{eq:dag}.
                Hence $G'$ is a DAG on $n-1$ vertices.

          \item \emph{Inductive hypothesis.}
                By the inductive hypothesis, $G'$ admits a topological
                ordering $\tau' : V \setminus \{s\} \to \{1, \ldots, n-1\}$.

          \item \emph{Extending to $G$.}
                Define
                %
                \begin{align}
                  \tau(s)   &\;=\; 1, \label{eq:tau-source}\\
                  \tau(v)   &\;=\; \tau'(v) + 1
                    \quad \forall\, v \in V \setminus \{s\}.
                    \label{eq:tau-rest}
                \end{align}
                %
                $\tau$ is a bijection $V \to \{1, \ldots, n\}$.
                For any edge $(O_i, O_j) \in E$:
                \begin{enumerate}
                  \item If $O_i = s$: $\tau(s) = 1 \leq \tau(O_j)$
                        and equality only if $\tau(O_j) = 1$, which
                        requires $O_j = s$, but self-loops are excluded
                        by Definition~\ref{def:dep-graph} ($i \neq j$).
                        Hence $\tau(s) < \tau(O_j)$.
                  \item If $O_i \neq s$: $(O_i, O_j) \in E'$, so
                        $\tau'(O_i) < \tau'(O_j)$, giving
                        $\tau(O_i) = \tau'(O_i)+1 < \tau'(O_j)+1
                        = \tau(O_j)$.
                \end{enumerate}
                Thus condition~\eqref{eq:topo-order} holds for all edges.
        \end{enumerate}

  \item By induction, every DAG on $n \geq 1$ vertices admits a
        topological ordering.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~3 ($2 \Rightarrow 1$: Topological order implies DAG).}

Assume a topological ordering $\tau$ satisfying~\eqref{eq:topo-order}
exists.  Suppose for contradiction that $G$ contains a directed cycle
$C = (O_{i_1}, O_{i_2}, \ldots, O_{i_k}, O_{i_1})$ of length $k \geq 1$.
Then condition~\eqref{eq:topo-order} applied to each edge in $C$ gives:
\begin{align}
  \tau(O_{i_1}) &\;<\; \tau(O_{i_2}), \label{eq:cyc-1}\\
  \tau(O_{i_2}) &\;<\; \tau(O_{i_3}), \label{eq:cyc-2}\\
                &\;\;\vdots \notag \\
  \tau(O_{i_k}) &\;<\; \tau(O_{i_1}). \label{eq:cyc-k}
\end{align}
By transitivity of $<$ applied to~\eqref{eq:cyc-1}--\eqref{eq:cyc-k}:
\begin{equation}
  \tau(O_{i_1}) \;<\; \tau(O_{i_1}),
  \label{eq:cyc-contradiction}
\end{equation}
which is a contradiction.  Hence no directed cycle exists and $G$ is a DAG.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~4 (Correctness of Algorithms~\ref{alg:kahns-init}
and~\ref{alg:kahns-main}).}

We verify that the algorithms produce a valid $\tau$ when $G$ is a DAG
and detect a cycle otherwise.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Loop invariant.}
        At the start of each iteration of the \textbf{while} loop, the
        following hold:
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item Every vertex in $\tau\_list$ has all its predecessors
                already in $\tau\_list$.
          \item $\mathit{indeg}[v]$ equals the number of predecessors
                of $v$ not yet appended to $\tau\_list$.
          \item Every vertex in $Q$ has $\mathit{indeg}[v] = 0$
                with respect to the remaining graph.
        \end{enumerate}
        \emph{Initialisation:} $\tau\_list = [\,]$, so (a) is vacuously
        true.  The in-degree table is computed correctly in
        Algorithm~\ref{alg:kahns-init}, so (b) holds.  $Q$ is
        initialised to all vertices of in-degree zero, so (c) holds.

        \emph{Maintenance:} When vertex $u$ is dequeued and appended,
        each out-neighbour $v$ of $u$ has its in-degree decremented.
        If $\mathit{indeg}[v]$ reaches zero, $v$ is enqueued.
        Invariant (a) holds for $u$ because $u$ was in $Q$ only after
        all its predecessors had been removed (by (b) and (c)).
        Invariants (b) and (c) are maintained by the decrement-and-enqueue
        logic.

  \item \emph{Termination.}
        Each vertex is enqueued at most once (only when its in-degree
        first reaches zero) and dequeued at most once.  Hence the loop
        terminates after at most $n$ iterations.

  \item \emph{Completeness (DAG case).}
        By Step~2, every non-empty sub-DAG has a source vertex.
        Therefore whenever $Q = \varnothing$ and $\mathit{count} < n$,
        remaining vertices form a sub-DAG; but Step~2 guarantees a
        source exists, so such a vertex would have been enqueued.
        Hence if $G$ is a DAG, the loop terminates with
        $\mathit{count} = n$, meaning all vertices are ordered.

  \item \emph{Cycle detection (non-DAG case).}
        If $G$ contains a cycle $C$, the vertices in $C$ never reach
        in-degree zero (each vertex in $C$ always retains at least one
        predecessor in $C$ that has not been removed).  Therefore they
        are never enqueued and the loop terminates with
        $\mathit{count} < n$, triggering the \textsc{CycleDetected}
        flag.

  \item \emph{Validity of produced $\tau$.}
        For any edge $(u, v) \in E$, $v$ is enqueued only after $u$ is
        dequeued and appended to $\tau\_list$.  Hence $u$ appears before
        $v$ in $\tau\_list$, so $\tau(u) < \tau(v)$, satisfying
        condition~\eqref{eq:topo-order}.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~5 (Complexity Analysis).}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Initialisation (Algorithm~\ref{alg:kahns-init}):}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item Setting $\mathit{indeg}[v] = 0$ for all $v$: $O(n)$.
          \item Iterating over all edges to accumulate in-degrees:
                $O(|E|)$.
          \item Scanning all vertices to populate $Q$: $O(n)$.
        \end{enumerate}
        Total initialisation cost: $O(n + |E|)$.

  \item \emph{Main loop (Algorithm~\ref{alg:kahns-main}):}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item Each vertex is enqueued and dequeued at most once:
                $O(n)$ queue operations.
          \item For each dequeued vertex $u$, all out-edges of $u$
                are processed exactly once across all iterations:
                $O(|E|)$ total decrement operations.
        \end{enumerate}
        Total main loop cost: $O(n + |E|)$.

  \item \emph{Overall:}
        \begin{equation}
          T_{\mathrm{Kahn}}
          \;=\; O(n + |E|) + O(n + |E|)
          \;=\; O(|V| + |E|).
          \label{eq:complexity-final}
        \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~6 (Boundary and Edge Cases).}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Empty edge set} ($E = \varnothing$):
        Every vertex has $\mathit{indeg}[v] = 0$; all are enqueued
        immediately.  Any permutation of $V$ is a valid topological
        ordering.  The algorithm produces one such permutation.
  \item \emph{Single vertex} ($n = 1$):
        Handled by the base case in Step~2.  The algorithm returns
        $\tau(O_1) = 1$ in $O(1)$ time.
  \item \emph{Complete DAG} ($|E| = n(n-1)/2$, a total order):
        Exactly one topological ordering exists; the algorithm
        recovers it correctly since each vertex has a unique
        in-degree-zero moment.
  \item \emph{Disconnected graph:}
        Connected components are handled independently by the queue;
        correctness within each component follows from the above.
  \item \emph{Cycle of length one} (self-loop, $(O_i, O_i) \in E$):
        Excluded by Definition~\ref{def:dep-graph} ($i \neq j$).
        If such an edge were present, $\mathit{indeg}[O_i] \geq 1$
        permanently, and the algorithm would correctly raise
        \textsc{CycleDetected}.
\end{enumerate}

Combining Steps~1--6, both directions of the equivalence hold, the
algorithms are correct for all valid inputs and boundary conditions, and
the time complexity is $O(|V| + |E|)$.\qed
\end{proof}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Idempotency and Referential Transparency}
\label{ch:idempotency}
% ==============================================================

\noindent
This chapter formalises two semantic properties---referential transparency
and idempotency---that are prerequisites for safe memoisation,
parallelisation, and fault-tolerant replay of operators within the
platform.  Every assumption is stated explicitly; every implication is
derived from first principles; all boundary conditions and degenerate
cases are addressed.

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Foundational Assumptions and Notation}
\label{sec:idemp-assumptions}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\noindent
Throughout this chapter the following assumptions hold.  Each is
validated in the subsequent proofs before it is used.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{(A1) State space.}
        $\calS$ is a non-empty set of platform states.  Each element
        $s \in \calS$ represents a complete, observable snapshot of
        the system at a given instant.

  \item \textbf{(A2) Information set.}
        $\calI_t$ denotes the totality of information available at
        time $t \in \calT$, where $\calT \subseteq \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$
        is a totally ordered index set.  $\calI_t$ is fixed and
        exogenous to the operator being evaluated.

  \item \textbf{(A3) Operator signature.}
        An operator $O : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ maps a state
        and an information set to a new state.  $O$ is a total function
        (defined for every element of its domain).

  \item \textbf{(A4) Equivalence relation.}
        The relation $\equiv \pmod{\calI_t}$ on $\calS$ is a valid
        equivalence relation: it is reflexive, symmetric, and
        transitive.  Formally, for all $s, s', s'' \in \calS$:
        \begin{align}
          &s \equiv s \pmod{\calI_t},
            \label{eq:equiv-refl} \\
          &s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}
            \;\Longrightarrow\;
            s' \equiv s \pmod{\calI_t},
            \label{eq:equiv-sym} \\
          &s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}
            \;\text{ and }\;
            s' \equiv s'' \pmod{\calI_t}
            \;\Longrightarrow\;
            s \equiv s'' \pmod{\calI_t}.
            \label{eq:equiv-trans}
        \end{align}
        Two states are equivalent modulo $\calI_t$ if they agree on
        every component observable under $\calI_t$.

  \item \textbf{(A5) Determinism.}
        For fixed arguments $(s, \calI_t)$, the operator $O$ returns a
        unique, deterministic output; there is no hidden non-determinism
        or dependence on mutable global state outside $\calI_t$.

  \item \textbf{(A6) No side effects on $\calI_t$.}
        Applying $O$ does not mutate $\calI_t$.  The information set is
        read-only from the perspective of any single operator
        invocation.

  \item \textbf{(A7) Composition closure.}
        If $O$ is applied to a state $s' = O(s, \calI_t)$, the result
        $s'$ belongs to $\calS$, so iterating $O$ is well-defined.
\end{enumerate}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Referential Transparency}
\label{sec:ref-transparency}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[Referential Transparency]
\label{def:ref-transparency}
Under Assumptions (A1)--(A6), an operator
$O : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ is
\emph{referentially transparent with respect to $\calI_t$} if, for
every pair of states $s, s' \in \calS$ that are indistinguishable
under the available information $\calI_t$,
\begin{equation}
  s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}
  \;\Longrightarrow\;
  O(s,\, \calI_t) \;=\; O(s',\, \calI_t).
  \label{eq:ref-trans}
\end{equation}
That is, the output of $O$ is determined entirely by the equivalence
class $[s]_{\calI_t}$ and the information set $\calI_t$; it does not
depend on aspects of $s$ that are invisible under $\calI_t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Degenerate Cases for Referential Transparency]
\label{rem:rt-degenerate}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Trivial equivalence} ($s = s'$):
        Equation~\eqref{eq:ref-trans} reduces to
        $O(s, \calI_t) = O(s, \calI_t)$, which holds by determinism
        (A5).
  \item \emph{Discrete $\calI_t$} (every state is its own class):
        The antecedent $s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}$ implies $s = s'$,
        so~\eqref{eq:ref-trans} is vacuously satisfied for $s \neq s'$
        and trivially satisfied for $s = s'$.
  \item \emph{Universal $\calI_t$} (all states equivalent):
        Every pair satisfies the antecedent; the definition then
        requires that $O$ is a constant function on $\calS$ for fixed
        $\calI_t$.  This is the most restrictive boundary case.
  \item \emph{Empty state space} ($\calS = \varnothing$):
        The condition is vacuously true; no operator can violate it.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Referential Transparency Is Preserved under
  Composition]
\label{prop:rt-composition}
Let $O_1$ and $O_2$ both satisfy Definition~\ref{def:ref-transparency}
with respect to $\calI_t$.  Define the composed operator
$O_{12}(s, \calI_t) := O_2(O_1(s, \calI_t),\, \calI_t)$.
Then $O_{12}$ is also referentially transparent with respect to
$\calI_t$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We verify every assumption and proceed step by step without omission.
Let $s, s' \in \calS$ and $t \in \calT$ be arbitrary but fixed
throughout the proof.  We establish the conclusion
$O_{12}(s,\calI_t) = O_{12}(s',\calI_t)$ under the sole hypothesis
$s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}$.

\begin{enumerate}

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Validation of the hypothesis domain.}
        By Assumption~(A1), $\calS \neq \varnothing$ and
        $\calT \neq \varnothing$, so the choice of $s, s' \in \calS$
        and $t \in \calT$ is non-vacuous.  By Assumption~(A2),
        $\calI_t \in \calI$ is well-defined for every $t \in \calT$.
        The equivalence relation $\equiv_{\calI_t}$ on $\calS$ is
        guaranteed by Assumption~(A4) (reflexivity, symmetry,
        transitivity), so the hypothesis
        \begin{equation}
          s \;\equiv\; s' \pmod{\calI_t}
          \label{eq:rt-comp-hyp}
        \end{equation}
        is a well-formed statement.  We assume~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-hyp}
        holds for the remainder of the proof.

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Well-typedness of $O_1$ and $O_2$.}
        By Assumption~(A3), both operators
        $O_1 : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ and
        $O_2 : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ are total functions.
        Hence $O_1(s, \calI_t)$, $O_1(s', \calI_t)$,
        $O_2(\cdot, \calI_t)$ are all well-defined elements of
        $\calS$ for any argument in $\calS$.

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Application of referential transparency of $O_1$.}
        By hypothesis, $O_1$ satisfies
        Definition~\ref{def:ref-transparency} with respect to
        $\calI_t$.  Applying that definition to the
        hypothesis~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-hyp} yields:
        \begin{equation}
          O_1(s,\, \calI_t)
          \;=\;
          O_1(s',\, \calI_t).
          \label{eq:rt-comp-step1}
        \end{equation}
        This step is valid because: (i)~$s, s' \in \calS$ (Step~1),
        (ii)~$\calI_t \in \calI$ (Step~1), and
        (iii)~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-hyp} provides the required antecedent.

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Introduction of intermediate states.}
        Define
        \begin{align}
          s_1  &:= O_1(s,\, \calI_t)  \;\in\; \calS,
          \label{eq:rt-comp-s1}\\
          s_1' &:= O_1(s',\, \calI_t) \;\in\; \calS.
          \label{eq:rt-comp-s1p}
        \end{align}
        The membership $s_1, s_1' \in \calS$ follows from the
        co-domain of $O_1$ established in Step~2.
        By~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-step1}:
        \begin{equation}
          s_1 \;=\; s_1'.
          \label{eq:rt-comp-eq-s1}
        \end{equation}

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Lifting equality to equivalence for $O_2$.}
        Equation~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-eq-s1} states $s_1 = s_1'$
        (propositional equality in $\calS$).  By reflexivity of
        $\equiv_{\calI_t}$ (Assumption~A4,
        equation~\eqref{eq:equiv-refl}) applied to $s_1 \in \calS$:
        \begin{equation}
          s_1 \;\equiv\; s_1 \pmod{\calI_t}.
          \label{eq:rt-comp-refl-s1}
        \end{equation}
        Since $s_1' = s_1$ by~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-eq-s1}, substitution
        into~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-refl-s1} gives:
        \begin{equation}
          s_1 \;\equiv\; s_1' \pmod{\calI_t}.
          \label{eq:rt-comp-step2}
        \end{equation}
        This provides the antecedent required by the referential
        transparency of $O_2$.

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Validation that $s_1$ and $s_1'$ satisfy the
        domain of $O_2$.}
        By Step~2, $O_2 : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ is total.
        We have $s_1, s_1' \in \calS$ (Step~4) and
        $\calI_t \in \calI$ (Step~1), so $O_2(s_1, \calI_t)$ and
        $O_2(s_1', \calI_t)$ are both well-defined elements of
        $\calS$.  No additional assumptions are needed.

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Application of referential transparency of $O_2$.}
        By hypothesis, $O_2$ satisfies
        Definition~\ref{def:ref-transparency} with respect to
        $\calI_t$.  Applying that definition to~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-step2}:
        \begin{equation}
          O_2(s_1,\, \calI_t)
          \;=\;
          O_2(s_1',\, \calI_t).
          \label{eq:rt-comp-step3}
        \end{equation}
        The antecedent~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-step2} is satisfied (Step~5);
        the domain conditions are satisfied (Step~6).

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Unfolding the definition of $O_{12}$ on the
        left-hand side.}
        By the definition of the composed operator
        $O_{12}(s, \calI_t) := O_2(O_1(s, \calI_t), \calI_t)$
        and the abbreviations~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-s1}:
        \begin{align}
          O_{12}(s,\, \calI_t)
            &\;:=\; O_2\!\left(O_1(s,\, \calI_t),\, \calI_t\right)
          \label{eq:rt-comp-lhs-a}\\
            &\;=\;   O_2(s_1,\, \calI_t).
          \label{eq:rt-comp-lhs-b}
        \end{align}
        Step~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-lhs-b} follows by substituting the
        definition~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-s1} of $s_1$.

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Unfolding the definition of $O_{12}$ on the
        right-hand side.}
        By the definition of $O_{12}$ and~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-s1p}:
        \begin{align}
          O_{12}(s',\, \calI_t)
            &\;:=\; O_2\!\left(O_1(s',\, \calI_t),\, \calI_t\right)
          \label{eq:rt-comp-rhs-a}\\
            &\;=\;   O_2(s_1',\, \calI_t).
          \label{eq:rt-comp-rhs-b}
        \end{align}

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Combining all steps to conclude.}
        Chaining \eqref{eq:rt-comp-lhs-b}, \eqref{eq:rt-comp-step3},
        and~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-rhs-b}:
        \begin{align}
          O_{12}(s,\, \calI_t)
            &\;=\; O_2(s_1,\, \calI_t)
              &&\text{by \eqref{eq:rt-comp-lhs-b}}
          \label{eq:rt-comp-chain1}\\
            &\;=\; O_2(s_1',\, \calI_t)
              &&\text{by \eqref{eq:rt-comp-step3}}
          \label{eq:rt-comp-chain2}\\
            &\;=\; O_{12}(s',\, \calI_t).
              &&\text{by \eqref{eq:rt-comp-rhs-b}}
          \label{eq:rt-comp-conclude}
        \end{align}

  %% ---------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Boundary and edge-case audit.}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item \emph{$s = s'$:}
                Equation~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-conclude} reduces to
                $O_{12}(s,\calI_t) = O_{12}(s,\calI_t)$, which holds
                by Assumption~(A5) (determinism).
          \item \emph{$\calS$ is a singleton} ($\calS = \{s_0\}$):
                Both $s = s' = s_0$, the equivalence holds trivially,
                and $O_{12}(s_0,\calI_t) = O_{12}(s_0,\calI_t)$.
          \item \emph{Discrete partition} (every state is its own
                equivalence class under $\calI_t$):
                The antecedent $s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}$ forces
                $s = s'$; the conclusion then follows by the $s = s'$
                case above.
          \item \emph{Universal partition} (all states equivalent
                under $\calI_t$): Every pair $(s,s')$ satisfies
                the antecedent.  The argument of Steps~3--10
                applies without modification.
          \item \emph{$O_1$ or $O_2$ is the identity operator:}
                If $O_1(s,\calI_t) = s$ for all $s$, then
                $s_1 = s$ and $s_1' = s'$, and the proof specialises
                to the referential transparency of $O_2$ alone, which
                holds by hypothesis.  The symmetric case is analogous.
          \item \emph{$O_1 = O_2 = O$:}
                The composed operator becomes $O_{12}(s,\calI_t)
                = O(O(s,\calI_t),\calI_t)$.  Steps~3--10 apply
                with $O_1$ and $O_2$ both instantiated to $O$, and
                the conclusion follows identically.
        \end{enumerate}
