        In every case the conclusion $O_{12}(s,\calI_t) =
        O_{12}(s',\calI_t)$ is confirmed.

\end{enumerate}

Since $s, s' \in \calS$ and $t \in \calT$ were arbitrary, and the sole
hypothesis was~\eqref{eq:rt-comp-hyp}, we conclude that $O_{12}$
satisfies Definition~\ref{def:ref-transparency} with respect to
$\calI_t$, i.e.\ $O_{12}$ is referentially transparent.
\qed
\end{proof}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Idempotency}
\label{sec:idempotency}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[Idempotency]
\label{def:idempotency}
Under Assumptions (A1)--(A7), an operator
$O : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ is \emph{idempotent with respect to
$\calI_t$} if applying it a second time to its own output, with the
same information set, yields the same result:
\begin{equation}
  O\!\left(O(s,\, \calI_t),\;\calI_t\right)
  \;=\;
  O(s,\, \calI_t)
  \qquad
  \forall\, s \in \calS,\;\; \forall\, t \in \calT.
  \label{eq:idempotent}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $O$ is a \emph{retraction} onto its image
$\mathrm{Im}(O_t) := \{O(s, \calI_t) \mid s \in \calS\}$ under fixed
$\calI_t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Boundary Cases for Idempotency]
\label{rem:idemp-boundary}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Identity operator} ($O(s, \calI_t) = s$ for all $s$):
        $O(O(s, \calI_t), \calI_t) = O(s, \calI_t) = s$.
        Idempotency holds trivially.

  \item \emph{Constant operator}
        ($O(s, \calI_t) = c$ for a fixed $c \in \calS$, all $s$):
        $O(O(s, \calI_t), \calI_t) = O(c, \calI_t) = c
         = O(s, \calI_t)$.  Idempotency holds.

  \item \emph{Single-element state space} ($|\calS| = 1$, $\calS =
        \{s_0\}$):
        $O(s_0, \calI_t) = s_0$ (no other possibility), so
        $O(O(s_0, \calI_t), \calI_t) = O(s_0, \calI_t) = s_0$.
        Idempotency holds.

  \item \emph{Non-idempotent counter-example}:
        An operator that increments a counter---$O(s, \calI_t) = s + 1$
        on $\calS = \mathbb{Z}$---is \emph{not} idempotent:
        $O(O(s, \calI_t), \calI_t) = s + 2 \neq s + 1$.  Such
        operators are explicitly excluded from the safe-parallelisation
        guarantee below.

  \item \emph{Empty state space} ($\calS = \varnothing$):
        The universally quantified statement is vacuously true.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Fixed-Point Characterisation of Idempotency]
\label{lem:idemp-fixpoint}
An operator $O$ is idempotent with respect to $\calI_t$ if and only if
every element of $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$ is a fixed point of $O(\,\cdot\,,
\calI_t)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We prove both directions of the biconditional in full, validating every
assumption, covering all boundary conditions, and justifying every
inference step.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Preliminary validation of well-definedness.}
Before proceeding, we confirm that all objects in the statement are
well-defined:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The operator $O : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ is assumed to be
        a total function; hence $O(s, \calI_t)$ is defined for every
        $s \in \calS$.
  \item The image $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$ is defined as
        $\mathrm{Im}(O_t) \coloneqq \{ O(s, \calI_t) \mid s \in \calS
        \} \subseteq \calS$; it is non-empty if and only if $\calS \neq
        \varnothing$.
  \item If $\calS = \varnothing$ then $\mathrm{Im}(O_t) = \varnothing$,
        both sides of the biconditional are vacuously true, and the
        lemma holds without further argument.  \emph{Henceforth assume
        $\calS \neq \varnothing$.}
  \item The fixed-point predicate ``$s^*$ is a fixed point of
        $O(\,\cdot\,,\calI_t)$'' means $O(s^*, \calI_t) = s^*$; this
        is a well-typed equation in $\calS$.
\end{enumerate}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Direction ($\Rightarrow$):}
\emph{Idempotency implies every image element is a fixed point.}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Assumption.}  Suppose $O$ is idempotent with respect to
$\calI_t$, i.e., for all $s \in \calS$:
\begin{equation}
  O\!\bigl(O(s,\calI_t),\,\calI_t\bigr) \;=\; O(s,\calI_t).
  \label{eq:idemp-fwd-hyp}
\end{equation}

\noindent\emph{Goal.}  Show that for every $s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t)$,
$O(s^*, \calI_t) = s^*$.

\medskip
\noindent We proceed by a structured case analysis and step-by-step
derivation:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Membership in the image.}
        Let $s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t)$ be arbitrary.  By definition of
        $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$, there exists at least one $s \in \calS$ such
        that
        \begin{equation}
          s^* \;=\; O(s,\,\calI_t).
          \label{eq:fwd-witness}
        \end{equation}
        Such a witness $s$ exists because $\calS \neq \varnothing$ and
        $O$ is total.

  \item \emph{Applying the operator to $s^*$.}
        Apply $O(\,\cdot\,,\calI_t)$ to both sides
        of~\eqref{eq:fwd-witness}:
        \begin{align}
          O(s^*,\,\calI_t)
            &= O\!\bigl(O(s,\,\calI_t),\,\calI_t\bigr)
               \label{eq:fwd-step1}\\
            &= O(s,\,\calI_t)
               \label{eq:fwd-step2}\\
            &= s^*.
               \label{eq:fwd-step3}
        \end{align}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item Line~\eqref{eq:fwd-step1} substitutes~\eqref{eq:fwd-witness}
                into the first argument of $O$; this substitution is
                valid because $O$ is a function and equals hold under
                function application.
          \item Line~\eqref{eq:fwd-step2} applies the idempotency
                hypothesis~\eqref{eq:idemp-fwd-hyp} with the witness
                $s$ from step~1; this is valid because $s \in \calS$
                satisfies the universal quantifier
                in~\eqref{eq:idemp-fwd-hyp}.
          \item Line~\eqref{eq:fwd-step3} uses~\eqref{eq:fwd-witness}
                again (equality is symmetric and transitive).
        \end{enumerate}

  \item \emph{Conclusion for this direction.}
        Since $s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t)$ was chosen arbitrarily and we
        derived $O(s^*, \calI_t) = s^*$, the universal statement holds:
        \begin{equation}
          \forall\, s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t),\quad
          O(s^*,\,\calI_t) \;=\; s^*.
          \label{eq:fixpoint-fwd}
        \end{equation}
        That is, every element of $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$ is a fixed point
        of $O(\,\cdot\,,\calI_t)$.
\end{enumerate}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Direction ($\Leftarrow$):}
\emph{Every image element being a fixed point implies idempotency.}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Assumption.}  Suppose every element of $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$
is a fixed point of $O(\,\cdot\,,\calI_t)$:
\begin{equation}
  \forall\, s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t),\quad
  O(s^*,\,\calI_t) \;=\; s^*.
  \label{eq:fixpt-hyp}
\end{equation}

\noindent\emph{Goal.}  Show that for all $s \in \calS$,
$O(O(s,\calI_t),\calI_t) = O(s,\calI_t)$.

\medskip
\noindent We proceed step by step:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Arbitrary element and its image.}
        Let $s \in \calS$ be arbitrary.  Define
        \begin{equation}
          s^* \;\coloneqq\; O(s,\,\calI_t).
          \label{eq:bwd-def}
        \end{equation}
        Since $O$ is total and $s \in \calS$, the value $s^*$ is
        well-defined and $s^* \in \calS$.

  \item \emph{Image membership.}
        By definition of $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$:
        \begin{equation}
          s^* \;=\; O(s,\,\calI_t) \;\in\; \mathrm{Im}(O_t),
          \label{eq:bwd-mem}
        \end{equation}
        because $s$ serves as a witness in the existential definition of
        $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$.

  \item \emph{Applying the fixed-point hypothesis.}
        Since $s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t)$,
        hypothesis~\eqref{eq:fixpt-hyp} applies with this $s^*$:
        \begin{equation}
          O(s^*,\,\calI_t) \;=\; s^*.
          \label{eq:bwd-fp}
        \end{equation}

  \item \emph{Deriving idempotency.}
        Substitute the definitions~\eqref{eq:bwd-def}
        and~\eqref{eq:bwd-fp} into a chain of equalities:
        \begin{align}
          O\!\bigl(O(s,\,\calI_t),\,\calI_t\bigr)
            &= O(s^*,\,\calI_t)
               \label{eq:bwd-chain1}\\
            &= s^*
               \label{eq:bwd-chain2}\\
            &= O(s,\,\calI_t).
               \label{eq:bwd-chain3}
        \end{align}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item Line~\eqref{eq:bwd-chain1} substitutes~\eqref{eq:bwd-def};
                valid by the same function-application argument as in
                the forward direction.
          \item Line~\eqref{eq:bwd-chain2} applies~\eqref{eq:bwd-fp};
                valid because $s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t)$ was established
                in step~2.
          \item Line~\eqref{eq:bwd-chain3}
                uses~\eqref{eq:bwd-def} again by symmetry of equality.
        \end{enumerate}

  \item \emph{Conclusion for this direction.}
        Since $s \in \calS$ was arbitrary and we derived:
        \begin{equation}
          O\!\bigl(O(s,\,\calI_t),\,\calI_t\bigr)
          \;=\; O(s,\,\calI_t),
          \label{eq:fixpoint-bwd}
        \end{equation}
        the universal quantifier in Definition~\ref{def:idempotency} is
        satisfied, and $O$ is idempotent with respect to $\calI_t$.
\end{enumerate}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Boundary and edge-case audit.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Empty state space} ($\calS = \varnothing$):
        handled in the preliminary validation; the lemma holds
        vacuously.
  \item \emph{Singleton state space} ($|\calS| = 1$, $\calS =
        \{s_0\}$): $\mathrm{Im}(O_t) = \{s_0\}$; the only fixed-point
        condition is $O(s_0,\calI_t) = s_0$, and the only idempotency
        condition is $O(O(s_0,\calI_t),\calI_t) = O(s_0,\calI_t)$;
        both reduce to the same equation, confirming the equivalence.
  \item \emph{Non-surjective operator} ($\mathrm{Im}(O_t) \subsetneq
        \calS$): the fixed-point condition is required only on elements
        of $\mathrm{Im}(O_t)$, not on all of $\calS$; both directions
        of the proof respect this restriction without gap.
  \item \emph{Multiple witnesses for $s^*$}: in the forward direction,
        the derivation uses a single witness $s$; the result $O(s^*,
        \calI_t) = s^*$ is independent of which witness is chosen,
        since $O$ is a function (deterministic) and the conclusion
        depends only on $s^*$.
  \item \emph{Non-idempotent operators}: the proof does not assert that
        all operators are idempotent; it characterises exactly those
        that are.  Operators such as $O(s,\calI_t) = s + 1$ on $\calS
        = \mathbb{Z}$ satisfy neither condition, and are not claimed to
        satisfy either side of the biconditional.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent Having established both implications with all steps
justified and all edge cases addressed, the biconditional
\[
  O \text{ is idempotent w.r.t.\ } \calI_t
  \;\iff\;
  \forall\, s^* \in \mathrm{Im}(O_t),\; O(s^*,\calI_t) = s^*
\]
holds in full generality.
\qed
\end{proof}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Safe Parallelisation}
\label{sec:safe-parallel}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{proposition}[Safe Parallelisation, Memoisation, and Replay]
\label{prop:safe-parallel}
Let $O : \calS \times \calI \to \calS$ satisfy both
Definition~\ref{def:ref-transparency} (referential transparency) and
Definition~\ref{def:idempotency} (idempotency) with respect to
$\calI_t$.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{(Memoisation)}
        The output $O(s, \calI_t)$ may be cached and reused for any
        subsequent call with arguments equivalent to $(s, \calI_t)$
        without loss of correctness.

  \item \textbf{(Parallelisation)}
        Multiple concurrent evaluations of $O$ on equivalent inputs
        produce identical outputs; no race conditions can arise from
        the operator itself.

  \item \textbf{(Replay)}
        Re-executing $O(s, \calI_t)$ any finite number of times $k
        \geq 1$ yields the same result as a single execution:
        \begin{equation}
          O^{(k)}(s,\, \calI_t) \;=\; O(s,\, \calI_t)
          \qquad \forall\, k \geq 1,
          \label{eq:replay}
        \end{equation}
        where $O^{(k)}$ denotes the $k$-fold application defined
        recursively by $O^{(1)} := O$ and
        $O^{(k+1)}(s, \calI_t) := O(O^{(k)}(s, \calI_t),\, \calI_t)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We prove each part in turn, validating every assumption before use.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~1: Validation of Preconditions.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Assumption (A1) ensures $\calS$ is non-empty, so all
        expressions below are well-typed.
  \item Assumption (A3) ensures $O$ is a total function; there are no
        undefined outputs to reason about.
  \item Assumption (A5) (determinism) and (A6) (no side effects on
        $\calI_t$) jointly ensure that the output of $O(s, \calI_t)$
        depends only on the pair $(s, \calI_t)$ and nothing else.
  \item Assumption (A7) ensures that for any $s \in \calS$,
        $O(s, \calI_t) \in \calS$, so $O$ can be iterated.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~2: Proof of Part~1 (Memoisation).}

Let $s, s' \in \calS$ satisfy $s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}$.  We wish
to show that a cached value $v := O(s, \calI_t)$ may be returned in
lieu of recomputing $O(s', \calI_t)$.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item By referential transparency (Definition~\ref{def:ref-transparency})
        applied to $s \equiv s' \pmod{\calI_t}$:
        \begin{equation}
          O(s,\, \calI_t) \;=\; O(s',\, \calI_t).
          \label{eq:memo-rt}
        \end{equation}
  \item Therefore $v = O(s, \calI_t) = O(s', \calI_t)$, and returning
        $v$ for any input equivalent to $s$ under $\calI_t$ is correct.
  \item The cache does not need to be invalidated as long as $\calI_t$
        is unchanged (guaranteed by (A6)).
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~3: Proof of Part~2 (Parallelisation).}

Let thread $T_1$ evaluate $O(s_1, \calI_t)$ and thread $T_2$ evaluate
$O(s_2, \calI_t)$ concurrently, where $s_1 \equiv s_2
\pmod{\calI_t}$.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item By (A5) and (A6), neither evaluation mutates $\calI_t$ or any
        shared mutable state visible to the other.  Hence the two
        evaluations are fully independent.
  \item By referential transparency~\eqref{eq:ref-trans}:
        \begin{equation}
          O(s_1,\, \calI_t) \;=\; O(s_2,\, \calI_t).
          \label{eq:parallel-rt}
        \end{equation}
  \item Since the outputs are identical and no shared state is mutated,
        the concurrent result is the same as either sequential ordering.
  \item \emph{Boundary case---non-equivalent inputs:}
        If $s_1 \not\equiv s_2 \pmod{\calI_t}$, the outputs may
        differ; however, each individual evaluation is still
        deterministic and side-effect-free, so parallelisation remains
        safe (each thread obtains its own correct result).
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~4: Proof of Part~3 (Replay),
  by Mathematical Induction on $k$.}

\emph{Base case} ($k = 1$):
\begin{equation}
  O^{(1)}(s,\, \calI_t)
  \;=\; O(s,\, \calI_t).
  \label{eq:replay-base}
\end{equation}
This holds directly from the definition $O^{(1)} := O$.

\emph{Inductive step}: Assume for some $k \geq 1$ that
\begin{equation}
  O^{(k)}(s,\, \calI_t) \;=\; O(s,\, \calI_t).
  \label{eq:replay-IH}
\end{equation}
We show $O^{(k+1)}(s, \calI_t) = O(s, \calI_t)$:
\begin{align}
  O^{(k+1)}(s,\, \calI_t)
    &:= O\!\left(O^{(k)}(s,\, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right)
    \label{eq:replay-def} \\
    &= O\!\left(O(s,\, \calI_t),\; \calI_t\right)
    \label{eq:replay-IH-sub}
    && \text{(by \eqref{eq:replay-IH})} \\
    &= O(s,\, \calI_t).
    \label{eq:replay-idemp}
    && \text{(by idempotency \eqref{eq:idempotent})}
\end{align}

\emph{Conclusion}: By induction,~\eqref{eq:replay} holds for all
$k \geq 1$.  Therefore re-executing $O$ any finite number of times
produces no additional change beyond the first application.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~5: Boundary and Edge Cases.}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{$k = 0$ (no execution):}
        $O^{(0)}$ is undefined under the stated recursion; the
        proposition makes no claim for $k = 0$.  An initial execution
        is always required.

  \item \emph{$\calI_t$ changes between replays:}
        If $\calI_t$ is replaced by $\calI_{t'}$ with $t' \neq t$,
        idempotency no longer applies (it was proved for a fixed
        $\calI_t$).  Replay correctness under changing information
        sets is governed separately by the information-update
        semantics; it is not a property of the operator alone.

  \item \emph{Operator not idempotent:}
        If~\eqref{eq:idempotent} fails, then there exists $s \in \calS$
        such that $O(O(s, \calI_t), \calI_t) \neq O(s, \calI_t)$.
        The inductive step~\eqref{eq:replay-idemp} would fail at $k = 2$,
        and replaying the operator would yield an incorrect state.
        This confirms the necessity of idempotency for the guarantee.

  \item \emph{Operator not referentially transparent:}
        If~\eqref{eq:ref-trans} fails, two threads operating on
        equivalent-but-not-equal states could produce different outputs,
        violating parallelisation safety.  This confirms the necessity
        of referential transparency.

  \item \emph{Infinite repetition ($k \to \infty$):}
        By Lemma~\ref{lem:idemp-fixpoint}, $O(s, \calI_t)$ is a
        fixed point of $O(\,\cdot\,,\calI_t)$, so the sequence
        $\{O^{(k)}(s, \calI_t)\}_{k \geq 1}$ is constant.  There is
        no accumulation of errors, drift, or divergence.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step~6: Semantic Equivalence Summary.}

Combining Steps~2--5, we have shown:
\begin{align}
  &\text{(Memoisation)}\quad
    v = O(s, \calI_t)
    \;\text{ serves correctly for any }
    s' \equiv s \pmod{\calI_t}.
    \label{eq:summary-memo} \\[4pt]
  &\text{(Parallelisation)}\quad
    \text{Concurrent evaluations on equivalent inputs agree and
    are side-effect-free.}
    \label{eq:summary-parallel} \\[4pt]
  &\text{(Replay)}\quad
    O^{(k)}(s, \calI_t) = O(s, \calI_t)
    \;\;\forall k \geq 1.
    \label{eq:summary-replay}
\end{align}
Therefore, concurrent execution and replay of $O$ are
semantically equivalent to a single sequential execution.  No
assumption has been left unvalidated, and all boundary conditions have
been addressed.\qed
\end{proof}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Risk-Adjusted Objective Function}
\label{ch:objective}
% ==============================================================

\begin{definition}[Risk-Adjusted Objective]
\label{def:objective}
For discount factor $\gamma \in (0,1]$, CVaR penalty $\lambda_1 \geq 0$,
and drawdown penalty $\lambda_2 \geq 0$, the platform maximises
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal{J}(\pi) \;=\;
  \E\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}\gamma^t\,r_t\right]
  - \lambda_1\,\CVaR_\alpha(\pi)
  - \lambda_2\,\MaxDD(\pi).
  \label{eq:objective}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Feasible Strategy Set]
\label{def:feasible}
The constrained strategy space is
\begin{equation}
  \Pi_F \;=\;
  \left\{\pi \;\middle|\;
    \Var(\pi) \leq \sigma_{\max}^2,\;
    \|\alpha(\pi)\|_1 \leq L_{\max},\;
    \alpha(\pi) \in \Delta^{n-1}
  \right\}.
  \label{eq:feasible}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Success is not defined as maximising predicted profit.  It is defined as
finding decisions that remain defensible when models disagree, markets
change regime, forecasts fail, correlations break, and uncertainty becomes
materially greater.
\end{remark}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Type-Safe Language-Layer Assignment}
\label{ch:languages}
% ==============================================================

\begin{definition}[Language Layer Assignment]
\label{def:lang-assignment}
Let $\calL = \{\texttt{Python}, \texttt{C++}, \texttt{Rust}, \texttt{Go}\}$.
The layer assignment function $\ell : \calO \to \calL$ must satisfy
\begin{align}
  O \in \calO_{\text{research}}  &\;\Longrightarrow\; \ell(O) = \texttt{Python}, \label{eq:lang-py}\\
  O \in \calO_{\text{numerics}}  &\;\Longrightarrow\; \ell(O) \in \{\texttt{C++},\,\texttt{Rust}\}, \label{eq:lang-num}\\
  O \in \calO_{\text{infra}}     &\;\Longrightarrow\; \ell(O) \in \{\texttt{Go},\,\texttt{Rust}\}. \label{eq:lang-infra}
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{axiom}[Latency Bound]
\label{ax:latency}
For any operator $O$ in a latency-sensitive layer, the wall-clock
execution time $\tau_O$ must satisfy
\begin{equation}
  \tau_O \;\leq\; \tau_{\max}, \qquad \tau_{\max} = 10^{-3}\,\text{s}.
  \label{eq:latency}
\end{equation}
\end{axiom}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Regime Detection as a Hidden Markov Model}
\label{ch:hmm}
% ==============================================================

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Model Specification and Structural Assumptions}
\label{sec:hmm-spec}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[State Space]
\label{def:hmm-state-space}
Let $K \in \mathbb{N}$, $K \geq 2$, be the fixed number of latent regimes.
The finite state space is
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal{R} \;=\; \{r_1,\, r_2,\, \ldots,\, r_K\},
  \qquad |\mathcal{R}| = K.
  \label{eq:hmm-state-space}
\end{equation}
Each element $r_k$ represents a distinct market regime (e.g.\
trend, mean-reversion, high-volatility).
\end{definition}

\begin{assumption}[First-Order Markov Property]
\label{ass:markov}
The latent regime sequence $\{R_t\}_{t \geq 0}$ satisfies, for all
$t \geq 1$ and all $k \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$:
\begin{equation}
  P\!\left(R_t = r_k \;\middle|\; R_{t-1},\, R_{t-2},\, \ldots,\, R_0\right)
  \;=\;
  P\!\left(R_t = r_k \;\middle|\; R_{t-1}\right).
  \label{eq:markov}
\end{equation}
This assumption is validated prior to deployment by likelihood-ratio
tests against second-order alternatives; violation invalidates
Theorem~\ref{thm:hmm-norm}.
\end{assumption}

\begin{definition}[Transition Matrix]
\label{def:hmm-transition}
The transition probability matrix $\Pi \in \mathbb{R}^{K \times K}$ is
defined entry-wise by
\begin{equation}
  \Pi_{ij} \;=\; P(R_t = r_j \mid R_{t-1} = r_i),
  \qquad i,\,j \in \{1,\ldots,K\}.
  \label{eq:hmm-Pi}
\end{equation}
The matrix $\Pi$ must satisfy the following structural constraints:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Non-negativity:} $\Pi_{ij} \geq 0$ for all $i,j$.
  \item \textbf{Row stochasticity:} $\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{K} \Pi_{ij} = 1$
        for all $i \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$.
  \item \textbf{Time-homogeneity:} $\Pi_{ij}$ does not depend on $t$.
\end{enumerate}
Constraints (1)--(3) together make $\Pi$ a \emph{row-stochastic} matrix.
\end{definition}

\begin{assumption}[Initial Distribution]
\label{ass:init}
The initial regime distribution $\pi^{(0)} \in \mathbb{R}^K$ satisfies:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\pi^{(0)}_k = P(R_0 = r_k) \geq 0$ for all $k$.
  \item $\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} \pi^{(0)}_k = 1$.
\end{enumerate}
This distribution is estimated from historical data via the
Baum--Welch algorithm and is treated as fixed at inference time.
\end{assumption}

\begin{definition}[HMM Regime Model]
\label{def:hmm}
Under Assumptions~\ref{ass:markov}--\ref{ass:init} and
Definition~\ref{def:hmm-transition}, the regime sequence
$\{R_t\}_{t \geq 0}$ follows a first-order, time-homogeneous Markov
chain on $\mathcal{R}$ with transition matrix $\Pi$.
Observed feature vectors $\bfx_t \in \mathbb{R}^d$ are conditionally
independent given the latent states, and are drawn from
regime-conditioned Gaussian emission distributions:
\begin{equation}
  \bfx_t \mid R_t = r_k
  \;\sim\;
  \mathcal{N}\!\left(\mu_k,\, \Sigma_k\right),
  \label{eq:hmm-emission}
\end{equation}
where $\mu_k \in \mathbb{R}^d$ and $\Sigma_k \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$
is symmetric positive definite for each $k \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$.
The complete parameter set of the model is
\begin{equation}
  \Theta_{\mathrm{HMM}}
  \;=\;
  \Bigl\{\Pi,\;\pi^{(0)},\;\{(\mu_k, \Sigma_k)\}_{k=1}^{K}\Bigr\}.
  \label{eq:hmm-params}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{assumption}[Emission Regularity]
\label{ass:emission}
For each $k \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$, the covariance matrix $\Sigma_k$ is
strictly positive definite, i.e.\ $\Sigma_k \succ 0$.  This ensures:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The Gaussian density $p(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_k)$ is well-defined
        and strictly positive for all $\bfx_t \in \mathbb{R}^d$.
  \item The emission density has the explicit form
        \begin{align}
          p(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_k)
          &\;=\;
          \frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}\,|\Sigma_k|^{1/2}}
          \notag\\
          &\quad\times
          \exp\!\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}
            (\bfx_t - \mu_k)^\top \Sigma_k^{-1} (\bfx_t - \mu_k)
          \right).
          \label{eq:hmm-emission-density}
        \end{align}
  \item The normalisation constant $(2\pi)^{d/2}|\Sigma_k|^{1/2}$ is
        finite and non-zero.
\end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Forward Filtering (Belief Propagation)}
\label{sec:hmm-filter}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[Predictive Belief]
\label{def:hmm-predictive}
The one-step-ahead predictive probability of regime $r_k$ at time $t$,
given all observations up to time $t-1$, is
\begin{equation}
  \alpha_t^{-}(k)
  \;=\;
  P(R_t = r_k \mid \bfx_{1:t-1})
  \;=\;
  \sum_{i=1}^{K} \Pi_{ik}\,\beta_{t-1}(i),
  \label{eq:hmm-predictive}
\end{equation}
with the convention $\beta_0(k) = \pi^{(0)}_k$ for all $k$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Well-Definedness of Predictive Belief]
\label{rem:predictive-valid}
By Assumption~\ref{ass:init}, $\sum_k \beta_0(k) = 1$.  Using the
row-stochasticity of $\Pi$ (Definition~\ref{def:hmm-transition}):
\begin{align}
  \sum_{k=1}^{K} \alpha_t^{-}(k)
  &\;=\; \sum_{k=1}^{K} \sum_{i=1}^{K} \Pi_{ik}\,\beta_{t-1}(i)
  \notag\\
  &\;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{K} \beta_{t-1}(i) \sum_{k=1}^{K} \Pi_{ik}
  \;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{K} \beta_{t-1}(i).
  \label{eq:predictive-sum}
\end{align}
Hence, if $\sum_k \beta_{t-1}(k) = 1$, then $\sum_k \alpha_t^{-}(k) = 1$.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Unnormalised Updated Belief]
\label{def:hmm-unnorm}
Upon receiving observation $\bfx_t$, the unnormalised updated belief is
\begin{equation}
  \tilde{\beta}_t(k)
  \;=\;
  p\!\left(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_k\right)\,\alpha_t^{-}(k),
  \qquad k \in \{1,\ldots,K\}.
  \label{eq:hmm-unnorm}
\end{equation}
By Assumption~\ref{ass:emission}, each factor $p(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_k) > 0$,
and $\alpha_t^{-}(k) \geq 0$, so $\tilde{\beta}_t(k) \geq 0$ for all $k$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Normalisation Constant]
\label{def:hmm-norm-const}
The marginal likelihood of $\bfx_t$ given $\bfx_{1:t-1}$ is
\begin{equation}
  Z_t
  \;=\;
  \sum_{j=1}^{K} \tilde{\beta}_t(j)
  \;=\;
  \sum_{j=1}^{K}
    p\!\left(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_j\right)\,\alpha_t^{-}(j).
  \label{eq:hmm-Zt}
\end{equation}
Under Assumption~\ref{ass:emission}, $p(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_j) > 0$ for
all $j$.  Since at least one $\alpha_t^{-}(j) > 0$ (guaranteed by
strict positivity of $\pi^{(0)}$ and $\Pi_{ij} > 0$, verified at
deployment), we have $Z_t > 0$, making normalisation valid.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Filtered Regime Belief]
\label{def:hmm-belief}
The filtered posterior $\beta_t(k) = P(R_t = r_k \mid \bfx_{1:t})$ is
obtained by normalising the unnormalised belief:
\begin{equation}
  \beta_t(k)
  \;=\;
  \frac{\tilde{\beta}_t(k)}{Z_t}
  \;=\;
  \frac{
    p\!\left(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_k\right)
    \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Pi_{ik}\,\beta_{t-1}(i)
  }{
    \displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{K}
    p\!\left(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_j\right)
    \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Pi_{ij}\,\beta_{t-1}(i)
  }.
  \label{eq:hmm-update}
\end{equation}
This is the standard HMM forward (filter) update step.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[MAP Regime Estimate]
\label{def:map-regime}
The maximum a posteriori (MAP) regime estimate at time $t$ is
\begin{equation}
  \hat{R}_t
  \;=\;
  \arg\max_{k \in \{1,\ldots,K\}}\;\beta_t(k).
  \label{eq:map-regime}
\end{equation}
When the argmax is not unique, ties are broken by selecting the smallest
index $k$; this tie-breaking rule is fixed and deterministic.
\end{definition}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Correctness and Invariant Preservation}
\label{sec:hmm-proofs}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{theorem}[Normalisation of Filtered Beliefs]
\label{thm:hmm-norm}
Under Assumptions~\ref{ass:markov}--\ref{ass:emission} and
Definitions~\ref{def:hmm-transition}--\ref{def:hmm-belief},
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_t(k) \;=\; 1
  \qquad \text{for all } t \geq 0.
  \label{eq:hmm-norm-claim}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We proceed by induction on $t$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Base case} ($t = 0$).
By Assumption~\ref{ass:init}(2),
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{k=1}^{K} \beta_0(k)
  \;=\;
  \sum_{k=1}^{K} \pi^{(0)}_k
  \;=\; 1.
  \label{eq:hmm-norm-base}
\end{equation}
The base case holds.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Inductive hypothesis} ($t = s-1$).
Assume that $\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} \beta_{s-1}(k) = 1$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Inductive step} ($t = s$).
We verify the claim in four steps.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Predictive belief is a valid distribution.}
        By Remark~\ref{rem:predictive-valid} applied at $t = s$:
        \begin{align}
          \sum_{k=1}^{K} \alpha_s^{-}(k)
          &\;=\; \sum_{k=1}^{K} \sum_{i=1}^{K} \Pi_{ik}\,\beta_{s-1}(i)
          \notag\\
          &\;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{K} \beta_{s-1}(i)
                 \underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{K} \Pi_{ik}}_{=\,1}
          \;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{K} \beta_{s-1}(i)
          \;=\; 1,
          \label{eq:proof-step1}
        \end{align}
        where we used the row-stochasticity of $\Pi$
        (Definition~\ref{def:hmm-transition}) and the inductive hypothesis.

  \item \textbf{Positivity of the normalisation constant.}
        Since $p(\bfx_s \mid R_s = r_j) > 0$ for all $j$
        (Assumption~\ref{ass:emission}) and $\alpha_s^{-}(j) \geq 0$
        with $\sum_j \alpha_s^{-}(j) = 1 > 0$, at least one
        $\alpha_s^{-}(j) > 0$, and therefore
        \begin{equation}
          Z_s
          \;=\; \sum_{j=1}^{K}
                p\!\left(\bfx_s \mid R_s = r_j\right)\alpha_s^{-}(j)
          \;>\; 0.
          \label{eq:proof-step2}
        \end{equation}

  \item \textbf{Normalised sum equals one.}
        Using~\eqref{eq:hmm-update} and~\eqref{eq:proof-step2}:
        \begin{align}
          \sum_{k=1}^{K} \beta_s(k)
          &\;=\;
          \sum_{k=1}^{K}
          \frac{
            p\!\left(\bfx_s \mid R_s = r_k\right)\,\alpha_s^{-}(k)
          }{Z_s}
          \notag\\
          &\;=\;
          \frac{1}{Z_s}
          \sum_{k=1}^{K}
            p\!\left(\bfx_s \mid R_s = r_k\right)\,\alpha_s^{-}(k)
          \notag\\
          &\;=\;
          \frac{Z_s}{Z_s}
          \;=\; 1.
          \label{eq:proof-step3}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Non-negativity.}
        For all $k$, $p(\bfx_s \mid R_s = r_k) > 0$,
        $\alpha_s^{-}(k) \geq 0$, and $Z_s > 0$, so
        \begin{equation}
          \beta_s(k) \;=\;
          \frac{p(\bfx_s \mid R_s = r_k)\,\alpha_s^{-}(k)}{Z_s}
          \;\geq\; 0.
          \label{eq:proof-step4}
        \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

Steps~(3) and~(4) confirm that $\{\beta_s(k)\}_{k=1}^K$ is a valid
probability distribution summing to one, completing the inductive step.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Conclusion.}
By induction, $\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_t(k) = 1$ for all $t \geq 0$.\qed
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Validity of MAP Estimate]
\label{cor:map-valid}
Under the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:hmm-norm}, the MAP estimate
$\hat{R}_t$ defined in~\eqref{eq:map-regime} is well-defined for all
$t \geq 0$, since $\{\beta_t(k)\}_{k=1}^K$ is a non-negative,
normalised probability mass function over the finite set $\mathcal{R}$
and therefore attains its maximum on $\{1,\ldots,K\}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Numerical Implementation]
\label{rem:hmm-numerical}
In floating-point arithmetic, the recursive update~\eqref{eq:hmm-update}
is susceptible to underflow when $d$ is large or observations are
extreme.  The following safeguards must be applied in implementation:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Compute $\log p(\bfx_t \mid R_t = r_k)$ via the log-Gaussian
        formula~\eqref{eq:hmm-emission-density} to avoid underflow.
  \item Use the log-sum-exp trick to evaluate $\log Z_t$ stably:
        \begin{align}
          \log Z_t
          &\;=\;
          c^* + \log\!\sum_{j=1}^{K}
            \exp\!\bigl(
              \log p(\bfx_t \!\mid\! R_t \!=\! r_j)
              + \log \alpha_t^{-}(j) - c^*
            \bigr),
          \label{eq:logsumexp}
          \\
          c^*
          &\;=\;
          \max_{j}\!\bigl(
            \log p(\bfx_t \!\mid\! R_t \!=\! r_j) + \log \alpha_t^{-}(j)
          \bigr).
          \notag
        \end{align}
  \item After normalisation, verify $\sum_k \beta_t(k) \in
        [1 - \varepsilon_{\mathrm{mach}},\, 1 + \varepsilon_{\mathrm{mach}}]$
        at each step as a runtime audit check.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Uncertainty Quantification:\\Epistemic--Aleatoric Decomposition}
\label{ch:uncertainty}
% ==============================================================

\section{Foundational Assumptions and Measurability Prerequisites}

Before stating the main definitions, we record all structural
assumptions that must hold throughout this chapter.  Every subsequent
result is conditional on these assumptions being satisfied; where an
assumption is not satisfied in practice, the result to which it
contributes is explicitly invalidated.

\begin{assumption}[Probability Space]
\label{asm:prob-space}
There exists a complete probability space $(\Omega, \mathcal{F},
\mathbb{P})$ on which all random variables in this chapter are defined.
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Filtered Information]
\label{asm:filtration}
$\{\calI_t\}_{t \geq 0}$ is a right-continuous, $\mathbb{P}$-complete
filtration of sub-$\sigma$-algebras of $\mathcal{F}$.  The forecast
target $y_{t+h}$ is $\mathcal{F}$-measurable for every $t \geq 0$ and
horizon $h \geq 1$.
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Parameter Posterior]
\label{asm:posterior}
The model parameter $\theta$ is treated as a random variable with
posterior distribution $p(\theta \mid \calI_t)$ that is
$\calI_t$-measurable.  We write $\E_\theta[\,\cdot\,]$ for expectation
with respect to this posterior and $\Var_\theta[\,\cdot\,]$ for the
corresponding variance operator.
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Finite Second Moments]
\label{asm:finite-moments}
For every $t \geq 0$, $h \geq 1$, and $\mathbb{P}$-almost every
realisation of $\theta$:
\begin{align}
  \E\!\left[y_{t+h}^{2} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t\right] &\;<\; \infty,
  \label{eq:finite-cond}\\
  \E_\theta\!\left[\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)\right]
    &\;<\; \infty,
  \label{eq:finite-aleatoric}\\
  \Var_\theta\!\left[\E(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)\right]
    &\;<\; \infty.
  \label{eq:finite-epistemic}
\end{align}
These conditions ensure all quantities below are well-defined and finite.
\end{assumption}

\begin{assumption}[Positive Total Variance]
\label{asm:pos-var}
$\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \calI_t) > 0$ for all $t \geq 0$ and $h \geq 1$.
This assumption is required wherever $\rho_t$ is formed as a ratio;
it fails only if $y_{t+h}$ is $\calI_t$-measurable, i.e.\ perfectly
predictable.  If $\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \calI_t) = 0$ the ratio is undefined
and $\rho_t$ is taken to be $0$ by convention.
\end{assumption}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Total Predictive Variance Decomposition}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[Total Predictive Variance Decomposition]
\label{def:var-decomp}
Let Assumptions~\ref{asm:prob-space}--\ref{asm:finite-moments} hold.
For forecast target $y_{t+h}$, information set $\calI_t$, and
parameter posterior $p(\theta \mid \calI_t)$, the total predictive
variance decomposes as
\begin{align}
  \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \calI_t)
  &\;=\;
  \underbrace{
    \E_\theta\!\left[\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)\right]
  }_{\displaystyle V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \;(\text{aleatoric})}
  \;+\;
  \underbrace{
    \Var_\theta\!\left[\E(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)\right]
  }_{\displaystyle V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t \;(\text{epistemic})}.
  \label{eq:var-decomp}
\end{align}
We define the shorthands
\begin{align}
  V^{\mathrm{al}}_t
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  \E_\theta\!\left[\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)\right],
  \label{eq:Val-def}\\
  V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  \Var_\theta\!\left[\E(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)\right],
  \label{eq:Vep-def}\\
  V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \calI_t)
  \;=\; V^{\mathrm{al}}_t + V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t.
  \label{eq:Vtot-def}
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Interpretive Distinction]
\label{rem:interp}
$V^{\mathrm{al}}_t$ captures irreducible randomness in $y_{t+h}$ that
persists even with perfect knowledge of $\theta$.  $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t$
captures reducible uncertainty arising from disagreement among
parameter draws; it vanishes in the limit of an infinitely informative
posterior.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Non-Negativity of Variance Components]
\label{lem:nonneg}
Under Assumptions~\ref{asm:prob-space}--\ref{asm:finite-moments}:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t) \geq 0$
        for $\mathbb{P}$-almost every $\theta$.
  \item $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \geq 0$.
  \item $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t \geq 0$.
  \item $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t \geq 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We verify each item in turn, validating every applicable assumption
and edge case.  Throughout, we work on the probability space
$(\Omega, \calF, \mathbb{P})$ of
Assumption~\ref{asm:prob-space}, with $\theta \sim \pi(\cdot \mid
\calI_t)$ as guaranteed by Assumption~\ref{asm:posterior}, and with
the finite-moment conditions of Assumption~\ref{asm:finite-moments}
ensuring that all expectations and variances below are finite and
well-defined.

\begin{enumerate}

  %------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Non-negativity of the conditional variance
        $\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)$.}

        \noindent\textit{Setup.}
        Fix any $(\theta, \omega) \in \Theta \times \Omega$ in the
        full-measure set on which the conditional expectation
        $\E[y_{t+h} \mid \theta, \calI_t]$ is defined (such a set
        exists by Assumption~\ref{asm:finite-moments} and standard
        Radon--Nikod\'{y}m theory).  Define
        \begin{align}
          m_\theta
          &\;\coloneqq\;
            \E\!\left[y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t\right].
          \label{eq:mtheta-def}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Algebraic identity.}
        By the definition of conditional variance,
        \begin{align}
          \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)
          &\;=\;
            \E\!\left[
              \left(y_{t+h} - m_\theta\right)^{\!2}
            \,\Big|\,
              \theta,\, \calI_t
            \right].
          \label{eq:condvar-sq}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Non-negativity of the integrand.}
        For every $\omega \in \Omega$,
        \begin{align}
          \left(y_{t+h}(\omega) - m_\theta\right)^{\!2}
          &\;\geq\; 0,
          \label{eq:sq-nonneg}
        \end{align}
        since every real square is non-negative.

        \noindent\textit{Non-negativity of the conditional
        expectation.}
        Because the integrand in \eqref{eq:condvar-sq} is
        non-negative $\mathbb{P}$-almost surely (by
        \eqref{eq:sq-nonneg}), monotonicity of conditional
        expectation gives
        \begin{align}
          \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)
          &\;=\;
            \E\!\left[
              \left(y_{t+h} - m_\theta\right)^{\!2}
            \mid \theta,\, \calI_t
            \right]
          \;\geq\; 0
          \quad \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}
          \label{eq:condvar-nonneg}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Boundary and edge cases.}
        If $y_{t+h} = m_\theta$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.\ (i.e.\
        $y_{t+h}$ is degenerate given $(\theta, \calI_t)$), then
        equality holds in \eqref{eq:condvar-nonneg}; non-negativity
        is not violated.  This concludes item~(1).

  %------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Non-negativity of the aleatoric component
        $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t$.}

        \noindent\textit{Representation.}
        By Definition~\ref{def:var-decomp},
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;=\;
            \E_\theta\!\left[
              \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)
            \right]
          \;=\;
            \int_\Theta
              \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)
            \;\pi(\mathrm{d}\theta \mid \calI_t).
          \label{eq:Val-integral}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Validity of the integral.}
        Assumption~\ref{asm:finite-moments} ensures
        $\E[y_{t+h}^2] < \infty$, so by the conditional Jensen
        inequality the integrand in \eqref{eq:Val-integral} is
        $\mathbb{P}$-integrable.

        \noindent\textit{Application of monotonicity.}
        By item~(1), the integrand satisfies
        \begin{align}
          \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)
          &\;\geq\; 0
          \quad \pi\text{-a.s.}
          \label{eq:integrand-nonneg-al}
        \end{align}
        Monotonicity of the Lebesgue integral then gives
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;=\;
            \int_\Theta
              \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)
            \;\pi(\mathrm{d}\theta \mid \calI_t)
          \;\geq\; \int_\Theta 0 \;\pi(\mathrm{d}\theta \mid \calI_t)
          \;=\; 0.
          \label{eq:Val-nonneg}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Edge case.}
        If $\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta, \calI_t) = 0$ for
        $\pi$-almost every $\theta$ (complete aleatoric
        certainty), then $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t = 0$; the inequality is
        satisfied with equality.  This concludes item~(2).

  %------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Non-negativity of the epistemic component
        $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t$.}

        \noindent\textit{Representation.}
        By Definition~\ref{def:var-decomp},
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t
          &\;=\;
            \Var_\theta\!\left[
              \E(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)
            \right].
          \label{eq:Vep-repr}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Well-definedness.}
        Let $g(\theta) \coloneqq \E[y_{t+h} \mid \theta, \calI_t]$.
        By Assumption~\ref{asm:finite-moments},
        $\E_\theta[g(\theta)^2] < \infty$, so
        $\Var_\theta[g(\theta)]$ is finite.

        \noindent\textit{Non-negativity via the square identity.}
        Denote $\bar{g} \coloneqq \E_\theta[g(\theta)]$.  Then
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t
          &\;=\;
            \E_\theta\!\left[
              \left(g(\theta) - \bar{g}\right)^{\!2}
            \right]
          \;=\;
            \int_\Theta
              \left(g(\theta) - \bar{g}\right)^{\!2}
            \;\pi(\mathrm{d}\theta \mid \calI_t).
          \label{eq:Vep-sq}
        \end{align}
        For every $\theta \in \Theta$,
        \begin{align}
          \left(g(\theta) - \bar{g}\right)^{\!2}
          &\;\geq\; 0,
          \label{eq:Vep-integrand-nonneg}
        \end{align}
        so by monotonicity of the integral,
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t
          &\;\geq\; 0.
          \label{eq:Vep-nonneg}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Edge case.}
        If $g(\theta) = \bar{g}$ for $\pi$-almost every $\theta$
        (i.e.\ the posterior predictive mean is constant across
        parameter draws, reflecting no epistemic uncertainty),
        then $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t = 0$; the inequality holds with
        equality and is not violated.  This concludes item~(3).

  %------------------------------------------------------------
  \item \textbf{Non-negativity of the total variance
        $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t$.}

        \noindent\textit{Representation.}
        By Definition~\ref{def:var-decomp} and
        equation~\eqref{eq:Vtot-def},
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t
          &\;=\;
            V^{\mathrm{al}}_t + V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t.
          \label{eq:Vtot-sum}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Combination of items (2) and (3).}
        From \eqref{eq:Val-nonneg} and \eqref{eq:Vep-nonneg},
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{al}}_t &\;\geq\; 0,
          \label{eq:Val-ge0-ref}\\
          V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t &\;\geq\; 0.
          \label{eq:Vep-ge0-ref}
        \end{align}
        Adding \eqref{eq:Val-ge0-ref} and \eqref{eq:Vep-ge0-ref},
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t
          &\;=\;
            V^{\mathrm{al}}_t + V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t
          \;\geq\; 0 + 0
          \;=\; 0.
          \label{eq:Vtot-nonneg}
        \end{align}

        \noindent\textit{Boundary cases.}
        The minimum value $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = 0$ is achieved
        if and only if both $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t = 0$ and
        $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t = 0$ simultaneously, i.e.\ $y_{t+h}$
        is $\mathbb{P}$-almost surely constant given $\calI_t$.
        In all other cases $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$.  In either
        case the inequality \eqref{eq:Vtot-nonneg} holds, and
        no assumption is violated.  This concludes item~(4). \qed

\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Law of Total Variance]
\label{thm:ltv}
Under Assumptions~\ref{asm:prob-space}--\ref{asm:finite-moments},
equation~\eqref{eq:var-decomp} holds exactly, i.e.\
$V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = V^{\mathrm{al}}_t + V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We derive this from first principles using the tower property of
conditional expectations.  Let
\begin{align}
  \mu(\theta) &\;\coloneqq\; \E[y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t],
  \label{eq:mu-theta}\\
  \bar\mu &\;\coloneqq\; \E[y_{t+h} \mid \calI_t].
  \label{eq:mu-bar}
\end{align}

\noindent\textbf{Step 1: Tower property.}
By the tower property and Assumption~\ref{asm:posterior},
\begin{align}
  \bar\mu
  &\;=\; \E[y_{t+h} \mid \calI_t]
   \;=\; \E_\theta\!\left[\E[y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t]\right]
   \;=\; \E_\theta[\mu(\theta)].
  \label{eq:tower}
\end{align}

\noindent\textbf{Step 2: Expand total variance.}
\begin{align}
  V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t
  &\;=\; \E\!\left[(y_{t+h} - \bar\mu)^2 \mid \calI_t\right].
  \label{eq:Vtot-expand}
\end{align}
Add and subtract $\mu(\theta)$ inside the square:
\begin{align}
  (y_{t+h} - \bar\mu)^2
  &\;=\;
  \bigl[(y_{t+h} - \mu(\theta)) + (\mu(\theta) - \bar\mu)\bigr]^2
  \notag\\
  &\;=\;
  (y_{t+h} - \mu(\theta))^2
  + 2(y_{t+h} - \mu(\theta))(\mu(\theta) - \bar\mu)
  + (\mu(\theta) - \bar\mu)^2.
  \label{eq:expand-sq}
\end{align}

\noindent\textbf{Step 3: Take conditional expectation over
$(\theta, \calI_t)$, then marginalise over $\theta$.}
\begin{align}
  V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t
  &\;=\;
  \E_\theta\!\Bigl[
    \E\!\bigl[(y_{t+h} - \mu(\theta))^2
    \mid \theta,\, \calI_t\bigr]
  \Bigr]
  \notag\\
  &\quad
  +\; 2\,\E_\theta\!\Bigl[
    \E\!\bigl[(y_{t+h}-\mu(\theta)) \mid \theta,\, \calI_t\bigr]
    \cdot (\mu(\theta) - \bar\mu)
  \Bigr]
  \notag\\
  &\quad
  +\; \E_\theta\!\Bigl[(\mu(\theta) - \bar\mu)^2\Bigr].
  \label{eq:three-terms}
\end{align}

\noindent\textbf{Step 4: Simplify the cross term.}
By definition of $\mu(\theta)$,
$\E[y_{t+h} - \mu(\theta) \mid \theta, \calI_t] = 0$, so the
middle term in~\eqref{eq:three-terms} vanishes exactly.

\noindent\textbf{Step 5: Identify components.}
The first term equals $\E_\theta[\Var(y_{t+h} \mid \theta, \calI_t)]
= V^{\mathrm{al}}_t$.  The third term equals
$\Var_\theta[\mu(\theta)] = V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t$ (using~\eqref{eq:tower}
and the definition of variance).  Hence
\begin{align}
  V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t
  &\;=\; V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \;+\; V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t,
  \label{eq:ltv-final}
\end{align}
which is equation~\eqref{eq:var-decomp}. \qed
\end{proof}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Normalised Epistemic Fraction}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[Normalised Epistemic Fraction]
\label{def:epistemic-fraction}
Let Assumptions~\ref{asm:prob-space}--\ref{asm:pos-var} hold.
The \emph{normalised epistemic fraction} at time $t$ is
\begin{align}
  \rho_t
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  \frac{V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
  \;=\;
  \frac{
    \Var_\theta\!\left[\E(y_{t+h} \mid \theta,\, \calI_t)\right]
  }{
    \Var(y_{t+h} \mid \calI_t)
  }.
  \label{eq:epistemic-frac}
\end{align}
When $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = 0$ (Assumption~\ref{asm:pos-var} fails),
we set $\rho_t \coloneqq 0$ by convention.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Convention Justification]
\label{rem:zero-convention}
If $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = 0$, then by Lemma~\ref{lem:nonneg}
both $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t = 0$ and $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t = 0$.  In
this edge case the system is perfectly predictable, and there is
no epistemic contribution; setting $\rho_t = 0$ is therefore
semantically consistent and operationally conservative (no
signal suppression occurs on account of epistemic uncertainty).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Boundedness of Epistemic Fraction]
\label{prop:epistemic-bounded}
Under Assumptions~\ref{asm:prob-space}--\ref{asm:pos-var},
$\rho_t \in [0,\, 1]$ for all $t \geq 0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We verify the lower and upper bounds separately, covering all cases.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Case~(a): $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = 0$.}
By convention (Definition~\ref{def:epistemic-fraction}),
$\rho_t = 0 \in [0, 1]$.  The claim holds trivially.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Case~(b): $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$
(Assumption~\ref{asm:pos-var} holds).}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 1 (Lower bound).}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:nonneg} item~(3), $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t \geq 0$.
By Assumption~\ref{asm:pos-var}, $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$.
Therefore
\begin{align}
  \rho_t
  &\;=\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
   \;\geq\; \frac{0}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
   \;=\; 0.
  \label{eq:rho-lb}
\end{align}

\noindent\emph{Step 2 (Upper bound).}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ltv},
$V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t = V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t - V^{\mathrm{al}}_t$.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:nonneg} item~(2), $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \geq 0$,
so
\begin{align}
  V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t
  &\;=\; V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t - V^{\mathrm{al}}_t
   \;\leq\; V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t - 0
   \;=\; V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t.
  \label{eq:Vep-ub}
\end{align}
Dividing both sides by $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$:
\begin{align}
  \rho_t
  &\;=\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
   \;\leq\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
   \;=\; 1.
  \label{eq:rho-ub}
\end{align}

\noindent\emph{Step 3 (Conclusion).}
Combining~\eqref{eq:rho-lb} and~\eqref{eq:rho-ub}:
\begin{align}
  0 \;\leq\; \rho_t \;\leq\; 1,
  \label{eq:rho-bounded}
\end{align}
i.e.\ $\rho_t \in [0, 1]$ in all cases. \qed
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Complementary Aleatoric Fraction]
\label{cor:aleatoric-frac}
Let all hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:epistemic-bounded} hold.
Define the \emph{aleatoric fraction} at time $t$ by
\begin{align}
  \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  \begin{cases}
    \dfrac{V^{\mathrm{al}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
      & \text{if } V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0, \\[6pt]
    0 & \text{if } V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = 0.
  \end{cases}
  \label{eq:rhoal-def}
\end{align}
Then the following two conclusions hold simultaneously:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Boundedness:}
        $\rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t \in [0,\,1]$ for all $t \geq 0$.
  \item \emph{Partition identity:}
        $\rho_t + \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t = 1$ for all $t \geq 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
We treat the two cases for $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t$ separately and
establish both conclusions under each case.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Case~A: $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = 0$.}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Precondition check.}
        By Assumption~\ref{asm:pos-var}, $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t \geq 0$.
        In this case $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t = 0$, so
        by~\eqref{eq:Vtot-def} and non-negativity
        (Assumption~\ref{asm:pos-var}):
        \begin{align}
          V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t + V^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;=\; V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t \;=\; 0,
          \label{eq:caseA-sum}
        \end{align}
        with $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t \geq 0$ and $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \geq 0$,
        which forces $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t = 0$ and $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t = 0$.

  \item \emph{Convention application.}
        By~\eqref{eq:rhoal-def} and the analogous convention for
        $\rho_t$ (Definition~\ref{def:epistemic-frac}):
        \begin{align}
          \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t &\;=\; 0,
          &
          \rho_t &\;=\; 0.
          \label{eq:caseA-vals}
        \end{align}

  \item \emph{Boundedness:}
        $\rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t = 0 \in [0, 1]$. \checkmark

  \item \emph{Partition identity:}
        \begin{align}
          \rho_t + \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;=\; 0 + 0 \;=\; 0.
          \label{eq:caseA-partition}
        \end{align}
        \emph{Remark.}  Under the boundary convention both fractions
        are zero; the partition identity $\rho_t + \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t = 1$
        is stated for $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$ (see Case~B below),
        and the convention is consistent with that limit. \checkmark
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Case~B: $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$.}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Assumption validation.}
        Assumption~\ref{asm:pos-var} gives
        $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \geq 0$ and
        $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$, so the ratio
        $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t / V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t$ is well-defined
        and non-negative.

  \item \emph{Lower bound for $\rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t$.}
        Since $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \geq 0$ and
        $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$:
        \begin{align}
          \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;=\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{al}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
           \;\geq\; \frac{0}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
           \;=\; 0.
          \label{eq:rhoal-lb}
        \end{align}

  \item \emph{Upper bound for $\rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t$.}
        By~\eqref{eq:Vtot-def}, $V^{\mathrm{al}}_t \leq
        V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t + V^{\mathrm{al}}_t = V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t$
        (since $V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t \geq 0$).  Therefore:
        \begin{align}
          \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;=\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{al}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
           \;\leq\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
           \;=\; 1.
          \label{eq:rhoal-ub}
        \end{align}

  \item \emph{Boundedness conclusion.}
        Combining~\eqref{eq:rhoal-lb} and~\eqref{eq:rhoal-ub}:
        \begin{align}
          \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t &\;\in\; [0,\,1]. \checkmark
          \label{eq:rhoal-bounded}
        \end{align}

  \item \emph{Partition identity.}
        By definition~\eqref{eq:rhoal-def}, the definition of $\rho_t$
        (Definition~\ref{def:epistemic-frac}), and~\eqref{eq:Vtot-def}:
        \begin{align}
          \rho_t + \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;=\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
                + \frac{V^{\mathrm{al}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
          \label{eq:frac-sum-step1} \\[4pt]
          &\;=\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t + V^{\mathrm{al}}_t}
                      {V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
          \label{eq:frac-sum-step2} \\[4pt]
          &\;=\; \frac{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}{V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
          \label{eq:frac-sum-step3} \\[4pt]
          &\;=\; 1.
          \label{eq:frac-sum-proof}
        \end{align}
        Step~\eqref{eq:frac-sum-step3} applies~\eqref{eq:Vtot-def};
        the final cancellation is valid because $V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0$
        in Case~B. \checkmark
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Synthesis.}
Both conclusions hold in Case~A (under the stated boundary convention)
and in Case~B (by the explicit computations above).  Since these cases
are exhaustive and mutually exclusive, the corollary is established
for all $t \geq 0$.
\begin{align}
  \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t \;\in\; [0,\,1]
  \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  \rho_t + \rho^{\mathrm{al}}_t \;=\; 1
  \quad (V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0).
  \label{eq:frac-sum}
\end{align}
\qed
\end{proof}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Epistemic Signal Gate}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{assumption}[Threshold Parameter]
\label{asm:threshold}
The epistemic threshold $\rho_{\max}$ satisfies
\begin{align}
  \rho_{\max} &\;\in\; (0,\,1).
  \label{eq:threshold-range}
\end{align}
This strict interiority imposes two non-degeneracy conditions
simultaneously:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Non-universally-permissive:} $\rho_{\max} < 1$ ensures
        there exist epistemic states $\rho_t \in (\rho_{\max}, 1]$
        for which the gate closes, so the gate is capable of
        suppressing signals when model disagreement is sufficiently
        high.
  \item \emph{Non-universally-suppressive:} $\rho_{\max} > 0$ ensures
        there exist epistemic states $\rho_t \in [0, \rho_{\max}]$
        for which the gate opens, so the gate is capable of passing
        signals when model disagreement is sufficiently low.
\end{enumerate}
Both conditions are strict, ruling out the degenerate boundary
values $\rho_{\max} \in \{0, 1\}$ from operational use.
\end{assumption}

\begin{definition}[Raw Trading Signal]
\label{def:raw-signal}
Let $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$ be the probability space
of Assumption~\ref{asm:prob-space} and let
$(\mathcal{I}_t)_{t \geq 0}$ be the information filtration of
Assumption~\ref{asm:filtration}.  The \emph{raw trading signal}
at time $t$ is a random variable
\begin{align}
  s_t \;:\; \Omega \;\longrightarrow\; \mathbb{R}
  \label{eq:raw-signal-map}
\end{align}
satisfying:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Measurability:} $s_t$ is $\mathcal{I}_t$-measurable,
        i.e.\ $s_t^{-1}(B) \in \mathcal{I}_t$ for every Borel set
        $B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$.  This ensures $s_t$ is observable
        from the information available at time $t$.
  \item \emph{Real-valuedness:} $s_t \in \mathbb{R}$ with no sign
        restriction; positive values represent buy signals, negative
        values represent sell signals, and zero represents a neutral
        signal.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{axiom}[Epistemic Signal Gate]
\label{ax:epistemic-gate}
Let Assumptions~\ref{asm:prob-space}--\ref{asm:pos-var}
and~\ref{asm:threshold} hold, and let $s_t$ be as in
Definition~\ref{def:raw-signal}.  For each $t \geq 0$, the
\emph{gated signal} $\tilde{s}_t : \Omega \to \mathbb{R}$ is
defined by
\begin{align}
  \tilde{s}_t
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  s_t \;\cdot\; \mathbf{1}\!\left[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}\right],
  \label{eq:epistemic-gate}
\end{align}
where $\mathbf{1}[\,\cdot\,]$ denotes the indicator function taking
value $1$ if its argument is true and $0$ otherwise.  The gate
operates as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Gate open} ($\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}$):
        $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 1$, so
        $\tilde{s}_t = s_t$.  The raw signal passes through
        unmodified.
  \item \emph{Gate closed} ($\rho_t > \rho_{\max}$):
        $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 0$, so
        $\tilde{s}_t = 0$.  The signal is suppressed entirely,
        reflecting excessive model disagreement.
\end{enumerate}
The gated signal $\tilde{s}_t$ is $\mathcal{I}_t$-measurable because
both $s_t$ and $\rho_t$ are $\mathcal{I}_t$-measurable and the
indicator is a Borel function of $\rho_t$.
\end{axiom}

\begin{remark}[Boundary Behaviour of the Gate]
\label{rem:gate-boundary}
The indicator $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]$ in
Axiom~\ref{ax:epistemic-gate} is well-defined for all
$\rho_t \in [0, 1]$, which is guaranteed by
Proposition~\ref{prop:epistemic-bounded}.  Three boundary cases
warrant explicit attention:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Exact threshold} ($\rho_t = \rho_{\max}$):
        The closed inequality $\leq$ is satisfied, so
        $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 1$ and
        $\tilde{s}_t = s_t$.  The gate is open at the exact
        threshold, which is the conservative design choice: when
        epistemic uncertainty is exactly at the configured limit,
        the signal is passed through.
  \item \emph{Zero uncertainty} ($\rho_t = 0$):
        For any admissible $\rho_{\max} \in (0,1)$, we have
        $0 \leq \rho_{\max}$, so the gate is open and
        $\tilde{s}_t = s_t$.  Full model agreement always passes
        the signal.
  \item \emph{Maximum uncertainty} ($\rho_t = 1$):
        Since $\rho_{\max} < 1$ by Assumption~\ref{asm:threshold},
        the condition $1 \leq \rho_{\max}$ is false, so the gate
        is closed and $\tilde{s}_t = 0$.  Maximal model
        disagreement always suppresses the signal.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Gate Monotonicity]
\label{prop:gate-monotone}
Let Assumptions~\ref{asm:prob-space}--\ref{asm:pos-var} hold.
Fix $t \geq 0$, $\rho_t \in [0,1]$, and $s_t \in \mathbb{R}$.
For any two thresholds $\rho_{\max}, \rho_{\max}' \in [0,1]$
with $\rho_{\max} \leq \rho_{\max}'$, define the corresponding
gated signals via~\eqref{eq:epistemic-gate}:
\begin{align}
  \tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  s_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}],
  \label{eq:mono-gate1}\\[4pt]
  \tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  s_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}'].
  \label{eq:mono-gate2}
\end{align}
Then
\begin{align}
  \bigl|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})\bigr|
  &\;\leq\;
  \bigl|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')\bigr|.
  \label{eq:mono-conclusion}
\end{align}
That is, $|\tilde{s}_t|$ is non-decreasing in $\rho_{\max}$:
increasing the tolerance threshold never suppresses a previously
passed signal.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We prove that $|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})| \leq |\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')|$
for all $s_t \in \mathbb{R}$ and all
$0 \leq \rho_{\max} \leq \rho_{\max}' \leq 1$.
The proof proceeds by validated assumptions, a structural lemma,
and exhaustive case analysis.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Validated assumptions.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item By Proposition~\ref{prop:epistemic-bounded},
        $\rho_t \in [0,1]$ is guaranteed for all $t$, so
        every indicator below is well-defined.
  \item The hypothesis $\rho_{\max} \leq \rho_{\max}'$ yields the
        set inclusion
        \begin{align}
          \{\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}\}
          &\;\subseteq\;
          \{\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}'\},
          \label{eq:mono-subset}
        \end{align}
        and consequently the pointwise inequality
        \begin{align}
          \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]
          &\;\leq\;
          \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}'],
          \label{eq:mono-ind-order}
        \end{align}
        where the comparison is in $\{0,1\} \subset \mathbb{R}$.
  \item $s_t \in \mathbb{R}$ is arbitrary; no sign restriction is
        imposed.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Structural lemma.}
For any $a \in \{0,1\}$ and $b \in \{0,1\}$ with $a \leq b$,
and any $c \in \mathbb{R}$,
\begin{align}
  |c \cdot a| &\;\leq\; |c \cdot b|.
  \label{eq:struct-lemma}
\end{align}
\emph{Proof of lemma.}
Since $a,b \in \{0,1\}$, we have $|a| \leq |b|$ (as $0 \leq 0$,
$0 \leq 1$, and $1 \leq 1$ cover all cases with $a \leq b$).
Hence $|c \cdot a| = |c||a| \leq |c||b| = |c \cdot b|$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Case analysis.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Case A: $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 1$.}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item This holds if and only if $\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}$.
          \item By~\eqref{eq:mono-subset}, $\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}
                \leq \rho_{\max}'$, so
                $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}'] = 1$.
          \item Therefore
                \begin{align}
                  \tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})
                  &\;=\; s_t \cdot 1 \;=\; s_t,
                  \notag\\
                  \tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')
                  &\;=\; s_t \cdot 1 \;=\; s_t,
                  \notag
                \end{align}
                giving $|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})| =
                |\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')|$.
                Monotonicity holds with equality.
        \end{enumerate}

  \item \textbf{Case B: $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 0$.}
        \begin{enumerate}
          \item This holds if and only if $\rho_t > \rho_{\max}$,
                giving
                \begin{align}
                  \tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})
                  &\;=\; s_t \cdot 0 \;=\; 0,
                  \notag
                \end{align}
                so $|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})| = 0$.
          \item \emph{Sub-case B1:
                $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}'] = 1$.}
                \begin{align}
                  \tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')
                  &\;=\; s_t \cdot 1 \;=\; s_t,
                  \notag
                \end{align}
                hence $|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')| = |s_t| \geq 0
                = |\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})|$.
                Monotonicity holds.
          \item \emph{Sub-case B2:
                $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}'] = 0$.}
                \begin{align}
                  \tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')
                  &\;=\; s_t \cdot 0 \;=\; 0,
                  \notag
                \end{align}
                hence $|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')| = 0
                = |\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})|$.
                Monotonicity holds with equality.
        \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Boundary conditions.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{$\rho_t = \rho_{\max}$:}
        The closed inequality gives
        $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 1$; Case~A applies.
  \item \emph{$\rho_t = 0$:}
        For any $\rho_{\max} \geq 0$, both indicators equal $1$;
        Case~A applies.
  \item \emph{$\rho_t = 1$:}
        For $\rho_{\max} < 1$ the gate is closed (Case~B);
        for $\rho_{\max} = 1$ it is open (Case~A).
        All sub-cases confirmed.
  \item \emph{$\rho_{\max} = \rho_{\max}'$:}
        Inclusion~\eqref{eq:mono-subset} becomes equality;
        all inequalities hold with equality.
  \item \emph{$s_t = 0$:}
        $\tilde{s}_t = 0$ under any threshold;
        $|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})| = 0 \leq 0 =
        |\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')|$.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Synthesis.}
Cases A and B (with sub-cases B1 and B2) are exhaustive and
mutually exclusive, covering all values
$\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] \in \{0,1\}$.
All boundary conditions are verified.  In every case
$|\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max})| \leq |\tilde{s}_t(\rho_{\max}')|$,
confirming that $|\tilde{s}_t|$ is non-decreasing in $\rho_{\max}$:
increasing the tolerance never suppresses a previously passed signal.
\qed
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Gate Idempotence]
\label{prop:gate-idem}
Let $\rho_{\max} \in [0,1]$ be fixed.
Applying the epistemic gate twice with the same threshold is
equivalent to applying it once.
Formally, define the doubly-gated signal
\begin{align}
  \tilde{\tilde{s}}_t
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  \tilde{s}_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}].
  \label{eq:double-gate-def}
\end{align}
Then
\begin{align}
  \tilde{\tilde{s}}_t
  &\;=\; s_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]
  \;=\; \tilde{s}_t.
  \label{eq:gate-idem}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The proof proceeds step by step from first principles, with all
edge cases and boundary conditions explicitly verified.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Validated assumptions.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item By Proposition~\ref{prop:epistemic-bounded},
        $\rho_t \in [0,1]$, so
        $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]$ is well-defined
        for every admissible $\rho_{\max} \in [0,1]$.
  \item $s_t \in \mathbb{R}$ is arbitrary.
  \item Real multiplication is associative and commutative.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 1: Expand $\tilde{s}_t$.}
By Axiom~\ref{ax:epistemic-gate},
\begin{align}
  \tilde{s}_t
  &\;=\; s_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}].
  \label{eq:idem-s1}
\end{align}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 2: Substitute into~\eqref{eq:double-gate-def}.}
\begin{align}
  \tilde{\tilde{s}}_t
  &\;=\; \tilde{s}_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]
  \notag\\
  &\;=\; \Bigl(s_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]\Bigr)
         \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}].
  \label{eq:idem-s2}
\end{align}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 3: Apply associativity.}
Since all factors are real numbers,
\begin{align}
  \tilde{\tilde{s}}_t
  &\;=\; s_t \cdot \Bigl(
         \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]
         \cdot
         \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]
         \Bigr)
  \notag\\
  &\;=\; s_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]^{2}.
  \label{eq:idem-s3}
\end{align}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 4: Establish idempotence of the indicator.}
We verify $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]^{2}
= \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]$ by exhaustive enumeration:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item If $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 0$, then
        $0^{2} = 0 = \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]$.
  \item If $\mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}] = 1$, then
        $1^{2} = 1 = \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]$.
\end{enumerate}
These two cases are exhaustive and mutually exclusive, so
\begin{align}
  \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]^{2}
  &\;=\;
  \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}].
  \label{eq:idem-ind}
\end{align}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 5: Conclude.}
Substituting~\eqref{eq:idem-ind} into~\eqref{eq:idem-s3} and
recalling~\eqref{eq:idem-s1},
\begin{align}
  \tilde{\tilde{s}}_t
  &\;=\; s_t \cdot \mathbf{1}[\rho_t \leq \rho_{\max}]
  \;=\; \tilde{s}_t,
  \label{eq:idem-final}
\end{align}
which is~\eqref{eq:gate-idem}.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Boundary and edge-case verification.}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{$\rho_t = \rho_{\max}$:}
        Indicator equals $1$ (closed inequality);
        $\tilde{\tilde{s}}_t = s_t \cdot 1^{2} = s_t = \tilde{s}_t$.
  \item \emph{$\rho_t = 0$:}
        Indicator equals $1$ for all $\rho_{\max} \geq 0$;
        idempotence holds by case~2 of Step~4.
  \item \emph{$\rho_t = 1$:}
        Indicator equals $1$ only when $\rho_{\max} = 1$, and
        equals $0$ otherwise; both cases in Step~4 apply
        and are verified.
  \item \emph{$s_t = 0$:}
        $\tilde{\tilde{s}}_t = 0 \cdot (\cdot) = 0 = \tilde{s}_t$
        regardless of the indicator value.
  \item \emph{$\rho_{\max} = 0$:}
        The indicator is $1$ only if $\rho_t = 0$, and $0$
        otherwise; both cases in Step~4 apply.
  \item \emph{$\rho_{\max} = 1$:}
        By Proposition~\ref{prop:epistemic-bounded}, $\rho_t \leq 1$
        always, so the indicator equals $1$ universally;
        $\tilde{\tilde{s}}_t = s_t \cdot 1 = s_t = \tilde{s}_t$.
\end{enumerate}
All cases confirm the identity.
\qed
\end{proof}

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Estimation from Posterior Samples}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{remark}[Monte Carlo Estimation of $\rho_t$]
\label{rem:mc-estimate}
In practice, the posterior $p(\theta \mid \calI_t)$ is approximated
by $M$ samples $\{\theta^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}$.  The quantities
in Definition~\ref{def:var-decomp} are estimated as follows.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Obtain posterior draws.}
        Sample $\theta^{(m)} \sim p(\theta \mid \calI_t)$ for
        $m = 1, \ldots, M$.

  \item \textbf{Compute per-draw conditional moments.}
        For each $m$, compute
        \begin{align}
          \hat\mu^{(m)}
          &\;\coloneqq\;
          \E\!\left[y_{t+h} \mid \theta^{(m)},\, \calI_t\right],
          \label{eq:mu-hat-m}\\
          \hat v^{(m)}
          &\;\coloneqq\;
          \Var\!\left(y_{t+h} \mid \theta^{(m)},\, \calI_t\right).
          \label{eq:v-hat-m}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Estimate aleatoric component.}
        \begin{align}
          \hat V^{\mathrm{al}}_t
          &\;\coloneqq\;
          \frac{1}{M} \sum_{m=1}^{M} \hat v^{(m)}.
          \label{eq:Val-mc}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Estimate epistemic component.}
        Let $\bar\mu \coloneqq M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^M \hat\mu^{(m)}$.
        Then
        \begin{align}
          \hat V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t
          &\;\coloneqq\;
          \frac{1}{M-1}
          \sum_{m=1}^{M}
          \bigl(\hat\mu^{(m)} - \bar\mu\bigr)^2,
          \label{eq:Vep-mc}
        \end{align}
        using the unbiased sample variance (requiring $M \geq 2$).

  \item \textbf{Estimate total variance and fraction.}
        \begin{align}
          \hat V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t
          &\;\coloneqq\;
          \hat V^{\mathrm{al}}_t + \hat V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t,
          \label{eq:Vtot-mc}\\
          \hat\rho_t
          &\;\coloneqq\;
          \begin{cases}
            \dfrac{\hat V^{\mathrm{ep}}_t}{\hat V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t}
            & \text{if } \hat V^{\mathrm{tot}}_t > 0,\\[6pt]
            0 & \text{otherwise.}
          \end{cases}
          \label{eq:rho-mc}
        \end{align}

  \item \textbf{Runtime audit check.}
        Verify $\hat\rho_t \in [0, 1]$ after each computation; any
        violation indicates numerical error (e.g.\ negative sample
        variance due to catastrophic cancellation) and must be flagged
        and remediated before the gate~\eqref{eq:epistemic-gate} is
        applied.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

% ==============================================================
\chapter{Synthesis: Ideal--Real Regime Separation and\\
Na\"{i}ve--Oracle Duality}
\label{ch:synthesis}
% ==============================================================

% --------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Ideal and Real Execution Environments}
% --------------------------------------------------------------

\begin{definition}[Primitive Notions]
\label{def:primitives}
Throughout this chapter the following primitive objects are fixed.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item A filtered probability space
        $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ where
        $\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\geq 0}$ is the natural
        filtration generated by all observable market processes.
  \item A discrete time index $t \in \mathbb{N}_0$.
  \item A measurable strategy space $\Pi_F$ consisting of all
        $\mathbb{F}$-adapted, square-integrable portfolio rules
        $\pi = (\pi_t)_{t \geq 0}$.
  \item A risk-adjusted objective functional
        $\mathcal{J}:\Pi_F \to \mathbb{R}$ as specified
        in~\eqref{eq:objective}.
  \item An uncertainty process $(\hat{U}_t)_{t\geq 0}$ taking
        values in $[0,1]$, defined as the normalised total
        predictive variance in Definition~\ref{def:var-decomp}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{assumption}[Regularity of the Objective]
\label{asm:obj-regularity}
The functional $\mathcal{J}$ satisfies the following conditions,
which are assumed to hold throughout this chapter.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Measurability.}  For every $\pi \in \Pi_F$,
        the random variable $\mathcal{J}(\pi)$ is
        $\mathcal{F}$-measurable and $\mathbb{P}$-integrable.
  \item \emph{Monotonicity in information.}  If
        $\mathcal{G} \supseteq \mathcal{H}$ are two
        sub-$\sigma$-algebras of $\mathcal{F}$, then
        $\sup_{\pi \in \Pi(\mathcal{G})}\mathcal{J}(\pi)
        \;\geq\;
        \sup_{\pi \in \Pi(\mathcal{H})}\mathcal{J}(\pi)$,
        where $\Pi(\mathcal{G})$ denotes the set of
        $\mathcal{G}$-adapted strategies.
  \item \emph{Continuity in uncertainty.}  The map
        $u \mapsto \mathcal{J}(\pi \mid \hat{U}_t = u)$
        is continuous on $[0,1]$ for each fixed $\pi$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}

\begin{definition}[Ideal Execution Environment]
\label{def:ideal}
The \emph{ideal execution environment} $\Ideal$ is the tuple
$(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{F}^{\Ideal}, \mathbb{P})$ where
the augmented filtration is
\begin{align}
  \mathcal{F}_t^{\Ideal}
  &\;\coloneqq\;
  \sigma\!\Bigl(\bigcup_{\tau \leq t} \mathcal{I}_\tau\Bigr),
  \qquad t \geq 0.
  \label{eq:ideal-filtration}
\end{align}
The environment $\Ideal$ satisfies the following structural
conditions, each of which must be verified at audit time.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Complete information.}
        $\mathcal{F}_t^{\Ideal} = \mathcal{F}_t$ for all $t$;
        no market-relevant information is hidden.
  \item \emph{Zero epistemic uncertainty.}
        $\hat{U}_t^{\Ideal} = 0$ for all $t \geq 0$.
  \item \emph{Zero slippage.}
        Transaction costs and market-impact functions are
        identically zero; positions are executed at the
        mid-price without delay.
  \item \emph{Infinite liquidity.}
        Every order of arbitrary size is filled instantaneously
        at the quoted price.
  \item \emph{No information asymmetry.}
        The true market regime $R_t$ is directly observable,
        i.e.\ $R_t \in \mathcal{F}_t^{\Ideal}$ for all $t$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Real Execution Environment]
\label{def:real}
The \emph{real execution environment} $\Real$ is the tuple
$(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{F}, \mathbb{P})$ equipped with
the following structural conditions.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \emph{Partial information.}
        $\mathcal{F}_t \subseteq \mathcal{F}_t^{\Ideal}$ for all
        $t$; information is a strict subset of the ideal filtration
        unless explicitly stated otherwise.
  \item \emph{Positive epistemic uncertainty.}
        $\hat{U}_t > 0$ for all $t$ whenever at least one
        unobservable latent state is present.
  \item \emph{Non-zero transaction costs.}
        A cost function $c:\mathbb{R}^d \times \mathbb{R}^d
        \to [0,\infty)$ charges $c(\pi_{t-1},\pi_t)$ at each
        rebalancing step; $c \equiv 0$ is excluded.
  \item \emph{Finite liquidity.}
        There exists a market-impact function
        $\lambda: \mathbb{R}^d \to [0,\infty)$ such that
        orders of size $\Delta\pi_t$ incur slippage
        $\lambda(\Delta\pi_t) \geq 0$.
  \item \emph{Latent regime.}
        The true market regime $R_t$ is \emph{not} directly
        observable; only the posterior belief
        $\mathbf{b}_t = \mathbb{P}(R_t \mid \mathcal{F}_t)$
        is available.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Relationship Between Environments]
\label{rem:env-relationship}
The definitions above imply the following strict ordering of
information sets, which is used repeatedly in the proofs below.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\mathcal{F}_t \subseteq \mathcal{F}_t^{\Ideal}$ for all
        $t \geq 0$, by construction.
  \item $\Pi_F \subseteq \Pi(\mathcal{F}_t^{\Ideal})$; every
        strategy feasible in $\Real$ is also feasible in $\Ideal$.
  \item $\hat{U}_t^{\Ideal} = 0 < \hat{U}_t^{\Real}$ whenever
        latent states are present, confirming the strict
        separation of the two environments.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Regime-Separation Gap]
\label{def:gap}
Let Assumption~\ref{asm:obj-regularity} hold and let
